## [0501] delirium

**Cluster**: delirium | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Delirium is an acute, fluctuating disturbance of attention and awareness that is usually multifactorial and often preventable. Current guidance centres on team-based multicomponent prevention, and the ICU guideline update could not make a recommendation on antipsychotics to treat it.

### Core Teaching Points
- Prevention works best as a multicomponent, team-based package across the pre-, intra- and postoperative periods; the ESAIC 2024 update reports such packages reduce the incidence and duration of postoperative delirium. (PMID:37599617)
- Surgery-induced inflammation is pathogenic in postoperative delirium. Depth of anaesthesia, adequate analgesia and haemodynamic stability are modifiable by the anaesthetist, whereas length and site of surgery are not. (PMID:37599617)
- In ICU adults the 2025 SCCM focused update gave conditional recommendations for dexmedetomidine over propofol for sedation, enhanced mobilisation and rehabilitation over usual care, and melatonin. (PMID:39982143)
- The same SCCM update could not issue a recommendation on antipsychotics to treat delirium or on benzodiazepines to treat anxiety. (PMID:39982143)
- A 2025 meta-analysis found ramelteon, suvorexant and lemborexant associated with 40-46% relative reductions in delirium prevalence, but certainty was low and mortality and length of stay did not change. (PMID:40511987)
- The 2026 APA practice guideline contains 15 statements covering prevention, detection and treatment of delirium in adults. (PMID:41649238)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether antipsychotics have any role in treating established delirium (no ICU guideline recommendation).
- Comparative efficacy of individual sleep-wake agents: no credible subgroup differences, low-certainty evidence.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-line approach to delirium prevention: multicomponent non-drug package versus a single prescribed drug.
- ICU sedative choice and delirium: dexmedetomidine versus propofol or benzodiazepines.
- What the SCCM update did and did not recommend (melatonin yes, antipsychotic treatment no recommendation).
- Modifiable intra-operative contributors to postoperative delirium.

### Case Generation Hooks
- 78-year-old on day 2 after hip fracture surgery, newly inattentive and fluctuating overnight.
- Mechanically ventilated 70-year-old with agitation on a benzodiazepine infusion; which sedation change?

**Reviewer Gap**: Bedside diagnostic criteria (CAM/4AT) and a standard precipitant work-up are not covered by the retrieved abstracts; add from a diagnostic source before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37599617**: *Update of the European Society of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine evidence-based and consensus-based guideline on postoperative delirium in adult patients* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 39982143**: *A Focused Update to the Clinical Practice Guidelines for the Prevention and Management of Pain, Anxiety, Agitation/Sedation, Delirium, Immobility, and Sleep Disruption in Adult Patients in the ICU* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40511987**: *Efficacy of Ramelteon, Suvorexant, and Lemborexant for Delirium Prevention in Hospitalized Patients: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 41649238**: *The American Psychiatric Association Practice Guideline for the Prevention and Treatment of Delirium: Updated Implications for Patient Care* (2026) [guideline]

- ICU sedative choice and delirium: dexmedetomidine versus propofol or benzodiazepines.
- What the SCCM update did and did not recommend (melatonin yes, antipsychotic treatment no recommendation).
- Modifiable intra-operative contributors to postoperative delirium.

### Case Generation Hooks
- 78-year-old on day 2 after hip fracture surgery, newly inattentive and fluctuating overnight.
- Mechanically ventilated 70-year-old with agitation on a benzodiazepine infusion; which sedation change?

**Reviewer Gap**: Bedside diagnostic criteria (CAM/4AT) and a standard precipitant work-up are not covered by the retrieved abstracts; add from a diagnostic source before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37599617**: *Update of the European Society of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine evidence-based and consensus-based guideline on postoperative delirium in adult patients* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 39982143**: *A Focused Update to the Clinical Practice Guidelines for the Prevention and Management of Pain, Anxiety, Agitation/Sedation, Delirium, Immobility, and Sleep Disruption in Adult Patients in the ICU* (2025) [guideline]

## [0503] Delirium: recognition, risk factors, prevention and management

**Cluster**: delirium | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Delirium is an acute, fluctuating disturbance of attention and awareness that is usually multifactorial and often preventable. Current guidance centres on team-based multicomponent prevention, and the ICU guideline update could not make a recommendation on antipsychotics to treat it.

### Core Teaching Points
- Prevention works best as a multicomponent, team-based package across the pre-, intra- and postoperative periods; the ESAIC 2024 update reports such packages reduce the incidence and duration of postoperative delirium. (PMID:37599617)
- Surgery-induced inflammation is pathogenic in postoperative delirium. Depth of anaesthesia, adequate analgesia and haemodynamic stability are modifiable by the anaesthetist, whereas length and site of surgery are not. (PMID:37599617)
- In ICU adults the 2025 SCCM focused update gave conditional recommendations for dexmedetomidine over propofol for sedation, enhanced mobilisation and rehabilitation over usual care, and melatonin. (PMID:39982143)
- The same SCCM update could not issue a recommendation on antipsychotics to treat delirium or on benzodiazepines to treat anxiety. (PMID:39982143)
- A 2025 meta-analysis found ramelteon, suvorexant and lemborexant associated with 40-46% relative reductions in delirium prevalence, but certainty was low and mortality and length of stay did not change. (PMID:40511987)
- The 2026 APA practice guideline contains 15 statements covering prevention, detection and treatment of delirium in adults. (PMID:41649238)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether antipsychotics have any role in treating established delirium (no ICU guideline recommendation).
- Comparative efficacy of individual sleep-wake agents: no credible subgroup differences, low-certainty evidence.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-line approach to delirium prevention: multicomponent non-drug package versus a single prescribed drug.
- ICU sedative choice and delirium: dexmedetomidine versus propofol or benzodiazepines.
- What the SCCM update did and did not recommend (melatonin yes, antipsychotic treatment no recommendation).
- Modifiable intra-operative contributors to postoperative delirium.

### Case Generation Hooks
- 78-year-old on day 2 after hip fracture surgery, newly inattentive and fluctuating overnight.
- Mechanically ventilated 70-year-old with agitation on a benzodiazepine infusion; which sedation change?

**Reviewer Gap**: Bedside diagnostic criteria (CAM/4AT) and a standard precipitant work-up are not covered by the retrieved abstracts; add from a diagnostic source before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37599617**: *Update of the European Society of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine evidence-based and consensus-based guideline on postoperative delirium in adult patients* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 39982143**: *A Focused Update to the Clinical Practice Guidelines for the Prevention and Management of Pain, Anxiety, Agitation/Sedation, Delirium, Immobility, and Sleep Disruption in Adult Patients in the ICU* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40511987**: *Efficacy of Ramelteon, Suvorexant, and Lemborexant for Delirium Prevention in Hospitalized Patients: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 41649238**: *The American Psychiatric Association Practice Guideline for the Prevention and Treatment of Delirium: Updated Implications for Patient Care* (2026) [guideline]

## [0504] Delirium vs dementia differentiation

**Cluster**: delirium_vs_dementia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Delirium is acute and fluctuating while dementia is chronic and progressive, but the two coexist often: delirium superimposed on dementia is common in hospitalised older adults and carries worse outcomes than either alone.

### Core Teaching Points
- Delirium superimposed on dementia (DSD) is common in hospitalised older adults; compared with dementia alone it was associated with longer hospital stay, worse cognitive and functional outcomes, and higher institutionalisation and mortality. (PMID:34648761)
- Dementia is the leading risk factor for delirium, and hospital-, illness- and medication-related factors precipitate DSD. (PMID:34648761)
- Pre-existing cognitive impairment makes delirium harder to detect, and a reliable reference standard for diagnosing DSD does not yet exist. (PMID:27650668, PMID:33981143)
- Dementia with Lewy bodies has features of both delirium and dementia, which complicates diagnosis. (PMID:27650668)
- Among rapid bedside screens in older patients, two arousal tests (OSLA and RASS) had reproducibly high sensitivity and specificity; test-accuracy studies in DSD specifically were scarce. (PMID:29697753)
- Management of DSD includes managing distress and monitoring for resolution, with family involvement; persistent cognitive and functional decline and relapse are possible. (PMID:33981143)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Best validated screening tool specifically for DSD.
- Whether delirium exposure causes lasting neuronal injury in dementia (mechanism unclear).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Features favouring delirium over dementia: onset, course and attention.
- Outcomes of DSD compared with dementia alone.
- Why DLB is a diagnostic mimic of delirium.
- Which bedside arousal tests perform well in older inpatients.

### Case Generation Hooks
- 84-year-old with known Alzheimer disease admitted with UTI, now drowsy and agitated with new visual hallucinations.
- Family says an elderly relative changed abruptly over two days versus a slow decline over two years.

**Reviewer Gap**: Core distinguishing features (acute onset, fluctuation, inattention) are standard teaching but are not stated in the retrieved abstracts; confirm against DSM-5 or a guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27650668**: *The Diagnosis of Delirium Superimposed on Dementia: An Emerging Challenge* (2017) [review]
- **PMID 29697753**: *Attention, arousal and other rapid bedside screening instruments for delirium in older patients: a systematic review of test accuracy studies* (2018) [systematic review]
- **PMID 33981143**: *Current Challenges in the Recognition and Management of Delirium Superimposed on Dementia* (2021) [review]
- **PMID 34648761**: *Prevalence, Risk Factors, and Impact of Delirium on Hospitalized Older Adults With Dementia: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2022) [meta-analysis]

## [0505] dementia

**Cluster**: dementia_bpsd | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Agitation and other behavioural and psychological symptoms of dementia are common, disabling and drive caregiver burden. Recent consensus guidance prefers person-centred non-drug approaches first and flags the serious harms of sedating drugs.

### Core Teaching Points
- Aggression or agitation (39%), sleep problems (38%), eating problems (36%) and irritability (32%) were the most common BPSD in acute hospital settings in a 2025 meta-analysis, and BPSD were linked to delirium, pain and psychotropic use. (PMID:39888603)
- Because psychotropic drugs have modest efficacy and significant adverse effects, a 2026 consensus recommends person-centred care and non-pharmacological interventions as first-line treatment for agitation. (PMID:42563132)
- Benefit from individualised non-drug strategies was clearest when they followed a systematic person-centred assessment of the cause of the behaviour. (PMID:42563132)
- A 2026 primary-care consensus advises that treatment aim to reduce patient and caregiver distress and preserve function rather than sedate, and highlights harms of off-label drugs including excess mortality, cerebrovascular events, falls and sedation. (PMID:42771693)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Effect sizes of individual non-drug interventions (music, VR, robots) rest on small trials at risk of bias.
- The drug option with the best benefit-to-harm balance for severe agitation is not settled in these sources.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-line management of agitation in dementia: assess for causes and use non-drug approaches.
- Harms associated with antipsychotics in dementia.
- Common hospital BPSD and their link to delirium and pain.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Nursing-home resident with Alzheimer disease newly shouting and resisting care; what to look for before prescribing?
- Caregiver asking whether music or a sedative is better for evening agitation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Drug-specific recommendations (for example antipsychotic dosing or cholinesterase inhibitors) are not in the retrieved abstracts; add from NICE or APA dementia guidance. The existing DB mentor line citing WHO digital reminiscence therapy is a weak anchor.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39888603**: *Behavioural and psychological symptoms of people with dementia in acute hospital settings: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 42563132**: *Agitation in dementia: Evidence-based consensus recommendations* (2026) [guideline]
- **PMID 42771693**: *Targeted Treatment of Agitation in Alzheimer's Dementia: Prioritizing Function and Safety Over Sedation and Other Serious Harms* (2026) [guideline]

---

## [0508] Dementia with Lewy Bodies (DLB): core features, neuroleptic sensitivity management

**Cluster**: dlb | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Dementia with Lewy bodies is a common neurodegenerative dementia diagnosed by consensus clinical criteria supported by biomarkers; imaging with dopamine transporter SPECT and cardiac MIBG can help when the picture is uncertain.

### Core Teaching Points
- The fourth DLB Consortium consensus report refined recommendations for the clinical and pathological diagnosis of DLB and for management; DLB is now classified in DSM-5 as major neurocognitive disorder with Lewy bodies. (PMID:28592453)
- In mild cognitive impairment with Lewy bodies, attention, executive function and processing speed were worse than in MCI due to Alzheimer disease, while verbal immediate and delayed recall were better. (PMID:39791487)
- A 2026 Delphi consensus defines when striatal dopaminergic imaging and cardiac MIBG scintigraphy are useful in diagnosing dementia and MCI, and notes that comorbidities and medications can affect the biomarkers. (PMID:41797866)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Consensus statements reached agreement for only about half of items in each Delphi round.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Neuropsychological profile of Lewy body disease versus Alzheimer disease (attention and executive worse, recall relatively better).
- Role of dopamine transporter SPECT and cardiac MIBG in diagnosis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- 72-year-old with fluctuating cognition, visual hallucinations and parkinsonism; which investigation helps?

**Reviewer Gap**: Neuroleptic sensitivity, cholinesterase inhibitor evidence and REM sleep behaviour disorder are in the topic title but the retrieved abstracts do not state them; the DB-linked RCTs (rivastigmine, memantine) are kept for the reviewer to read.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28592453**: *Diagnosis and management of dementia with Lewy bodies: Fourth consensus report of the DLB Consortium* (2017) [guideline]
- **PMID 39791487**: *Neuropsychological test performance in mild cognitive impairment with Lewy bodies: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 41797866**: *Delphi consensus guidelines for the use of striatal dopaminergic imaging and cardiac metaiodobenzylguanidine (MIBG) scintigraphy for the diagnosis of dementia and mild cognitive impairment with Lewy bodies* (2026) [guideline]

## [0509] dengue

**Cluster**: dengue | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Dengue is a spectrum from self-limited febrile illness to severe plasma leakage. Recognising warning signs and secondary infection identifies who will progress, and NS1 testing is comparable to PCR in the first days of illness.

### Core Teaching Points
- A 2021 meta-analysis supports the WHO warning signs for predicting severe dengue and identified children, secondary infection, diabetes and renal disease as predictors; ascites, pleural effusion, hepatomegaly and melaena carried the highest odds. (PMID:34627388)
- Secondary dengue infection was a consistent risk factor for severe dengue in Latin America. (PMID:38263345)
- For acute diagnosis, pooled sensitivity was 95% for RT-PCR, 90% for NS1 ELISA (days 0-4) and 71% for IgM ELISA; IgM performs poorly early in illness, and NS1 matches RT-PCR accuracy at lower cost. (PMID:40209729)
- The 2025 WHO guideline provides integrated clinical management recommendations for dengue, chikungunya, Zika and yellow fever, which are hard to tell apart early without testing. (PMID:40690567)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Individual WHO recommendations (fluids, adjunct drugs) are not in the retrieved abstract; the DB-linked WHO 2026 montelukast statement needs checking before it is used.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Choice of test by day of illness: NS1/RT-PCR early, IgM later.
- Warning signs and risk factors for progression to severe dengue.
- Why secondary infection raises risk.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Day 4 of fever in a returning traveller: falling temperature, abdominal pain, rising haematocrit and falling platelets.

**Reviewer Gap**: Phases of illness and fluid management protocol are standard teaching but are not stated in the retrieved abstracts; add from the full WHO guideline.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34627388**: *Clinical predictors of severe dengue: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2021) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 38263345**: *Risk factors associated with severe dengue in Latin America: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40209729**: *Evaluating the performance of common reference laboratory tests for acute dengue diagnosis: a systematic review and meta-analysis of RT-PCR, NS1 ELISA, and IgM ELISA* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40690567**: *WHO guidelines for clinical management of arboviral diseases: dengue, chikungunya, Zika and yellow fever* (2025) [guideline]

## [0511] Denosumab, romosozumab, teriparatide: anabolic and resorption-inhibiting agents in osteoporosis

**Cluster**: osteoporosis_agents | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Anabolic agents (teriparatide, abaloparatide, romosozumab) and denosumab raise bone density and reduce fractures more than oral bisphosphonates in selected patients; the key practical issues are sequencing, rebound after stopping denosumab, and cardiovascular safety of romosozumab.

### Core Teaching Points
- In osteoporosis not previously treated with bisphosphonates, teriparatide reduced fracture risk compared with bisphosphonates (RR 0.61), and teriparatide and denosumab both raised BMD more than bisphosphonates. (PMID:39312040)
- Romosozumab produced larger early changes in lumbar BMD and bone turnover markers than denosumab, with greater effects within 12 months. (PMID:37529613)
- A network meta-analysis found no significant difference in cardiovascular mortality or major cardiovascular events between romosozumab and placebo or other agents, though real-world data are still needed. (PMID:39227560)
- PTH1 receptor agonists reduce all fractures versus placebo, with no difference in hip fracture; abaloparatide may offer more benefit than teriparatide for non-vertebral and hip fractures. (PMID:40047881)
- The 2024 RACGP/Healthy Bones Australia guideline addresses FRAX-based risk stratification, rebound vertebral fractures after denosumab cessation, clarifies imminent and very high fracture risk, and supports early use of osteoanabolic therapy. (PMID:40134107)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Cardiovascular safety of romosozumab in real-world high-risk patients.
- Optimal sequencing of anabolic and antiresorptive therapy is not specified in the retrieved abstracts.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Rebound vertebral fractures after stopping denosumab.
- Which agent is anabolic versus antiresorptive.
- Fracture reduction of teriparatide versus bisphosphonates.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Postmenopausal woman with two vertebral fractures and T-score -3.2: anabolic first or bisphosphonate?

**Reviewer Gap**: Contraindications (for example recent MI or stroke with romosozumab) are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37529613**: *Meta-analysis of the effects of denosumab and romosozumab on bone mineral density and turnover markers in patients with osteoporosis* (2023) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 39227560**: *Cardiovascular Safety of Romosozumab Compared to Commonly Used Anti-osteoporosis Medications in Postmenopausal Osteoporosis: A Systematic Review and Network Meta-analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 39312040**: *Efficacy and safety of teriparatide vs. bisphosphonates and denosumab vs. bisphosphonates in osteoporosis not previously treated with bisphosphonates: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40047881**: *PTH1 receptor agonists for fracture risk: a systematic review and network meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40134107**: *2024 Royal Australian College of General Practitioners and Healthy Bones Australia guideline for osteoporosis management and fracture prevention in postmenopausal women and men over 50 years of age* (2025) [guideline]

## [0512] depression

**Cluster**: depression | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Major depressive disorder is treated with antidepressants, psychotherapy or both, with escalation options when response is poor. Recent syntheses refine drug choice in adolescents, show limited added value of personalised brain stimulation and pharmacogenomic panels, and leave maintenance evidence for augmentation thin.

### Core Teaching Points
- In adolescents, a network meta-analysis ranked escitalopram highest for functional recovery and sertraline highest for clinician-rated severity and improvement. (PMID:40349006)
- In children and adolescents the dose-response curve for antidepressants was relatively flat, whereas discontinuation due to adverse effects rose sharply with higher doses. (PMID:40056173)
- Adjunctive atypical antipsychotics differ in efficacy and acceptability in MDD, and well-controlled studies of maintenance efficacy are lacking. (PMID:42090141)
- Personalised (imaging-guided) repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulation was not shown to be superior to fixed-target rTMS. (PMID:40194626)
- Pharmacogenomic-guided antidepressant selection shows a plateau in benefit with larger multigene panels, so cost-benefit and long-term remission outcomes need study. (PMID:40854804)
- A network meta-analysis ranked probiotics second after escitalopram and suggested possible use as adjunct or standalone therapy; this is emerging evidence rather than standard care. (PMID:38219239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term benefit of antidepressants versus psychotherapy: few studies report follow-up after treatment ends and outcome definitions differ.
- Value of augmentation with antipsychotics for maintenance.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Antidepressant choice in adolescents (escitalopram, sertraline).
- Whether pharmacogenomic panels or personalised rTMS improve outcomes (evidence does not clearly show benefit).
- Dose escalation of antidepressants in young people: flat efficacy, more discontinuation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- 16-year-old with moderate depression after CBT alone has failed: drug choice and monitoring.
- Adult with partial response to a first SSRI: switch, augment or add psychotherapy?

**Reviewer Gap**: First-line adult drug selection, treatment duration, suicide-risk monitoring, and anxiety-disorder content (topic 513) are not in the retrieved abstracts; add from NICE NG222 or APA guidance. Retrieval returned research-level syntheses, not a general guideline.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38219239**: *Probiotics for adults with major depressive disorder compared with antidepressants: a systematic review and network meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40056173**: *Antidepressant treatment of depression in children and adolescents: a systematic review and dose-response meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40194626**: *Effectiveness of personalized repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulation for major depressive disorder: A systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized active-controlled trials* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40349006**: *Comparative efficacy of antidepressant medication for adolescent depression: a network meta-analysis and systematic review* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40854804**: *Comparative effectiveness of pharmacogenomic-guided versus unguided antidepressant treatment in major depressive disorder: new insights from subgroup and cumulative meta-analyses* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 42090141**: *Adjunctive Antipsychotics in Major Depressive Disorder: A Systematic Review and Network Meta-Analysis* (2026) [meta-analysis]

---

**Reviewer Gap**: First-line adult drug selection, treatment duration, suicide-risk monitoring, and anxiety-disorder content (topic 513) are not in the retrieved abstracts; add from NICE NG222 or APA guidance. Retrieval returned research-level syntheses, not a general guideline.

## [0514] Depression: diagnosis, pharmacotherapy and monitoring

**Cluster**: depression | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Major depressive disorder is treated with antidepressants, psychotherapy or both, with escalation options when response is poor. Recent syntheses refine drug choice in adolescents, show limited added value of personalised brain stimulation and pharmacogenomic panels, and leave maintenance evidence for augmentation thin.

### Core Teaching Points
- In adolescents, a network meta-analysis ranked escitalopram highest for functional recovery and sertraline highest for clinician-rated severity and improvement. (PMID:40349006)
- In children and adolescents the dose-response curve for antidepressants was relatively flat, whereas discontinuation due to adverse effects rose sharply with higher doses. (PMID:40056173)
- Adjunctive atypical antipsychotics differ in efficacy and acceptability in MDD, and well-controlled studies of maintenance efficacy are lacking. (PMID:42090141)
- Personalised (imaging-guided) repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulation was not shown to be superior to fixed-target rTMS. (PMID:40194626)
- Pharmacogenomic-guided antidepressant selection shows a plateau in benefit with larger multigene panels, so cost-benefit and long-term remission outcomes need study. (PMID:40854804)
- A network meta-analysis ranked probiotics second after escitalopram and suggested possible use as adjunct or standalone therapy; this is emerging evidence rather than standard care. (PMID:38219239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term benefit of antidepressants versus psychotherapy: few studies report follow-up after treatment ends and outcome definitions differ.
- Value of augmentation with antipsychotics for maintenance.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Antidepressant choice in adolescents (escitalopram, sertraline).
- Whether pharmacogenomic panels or personalised rTMS improve outcomes (evidence does not clearly show benefit).
- Dose escalation of antidepressants in young people: flat efficacy, more discontinuation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- 16-year-old with moderate depression after CBT alone has failed: drug choice and monitoring.
- Adult with partial response to a first SSRI: switch, augment or add psychotherapy?

**Reviewer Gap**: First-line adult drug selection, treatment duration, suicide-risk monitoring, and anxiety-disorder content (topic 513) are not in the retrieved abstracts; add from NICE NG222 or APA guidance. Retrieval returned research-level syntheses, not a general guideline.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38219239**: *Probiotics for adults with major depressive disorder compared with antidepressants: a systematic review and network meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40056173**: *Antidepressant treatment of depression in children and adolescents: a systematic review and dose-response meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40194626**: *Effectiveness of personalized repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulation for major depressive disorder: A systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized active-controlled trials* (2025) [meta-analysis]

- **PMID 40854804**: *Comparative effectiveness of pharmacogenomic-guided versus unguided antidepressant treatment in major depressive disorder: new insights from subgroup and cumulative meta-analyses* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 42090141**: *Adjunctive Antipsychotics in Major Depressive Disorder: A Systematic Review and Network Meta-Analysis* (2026) [meta-analysis]

## [0515] De Quervain's Tenosynovitis

**Cluster**: de_quervain | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
De Quervain tenosynovitis is stenosing tenosynovitis of the first dorsal wrist compartment. A corticosteroid injection followed by a short period of thumb spica immobilisation is the best-supported first-line treatment, and ultrasound guidance improves results.

### Core Teaching Points
- A 2023 network meta-analysis supported corticosteroid injection followed by 3 to 4 weeks of immobilisation as first-line treatment; the extra benefit of adding immobilisation to injection was statistically but not clinically significant. (PMID:37889490)
- A 2024 network meta-analysis found corticosteroid injection with brief immobilisation remains the primary treatment, extracorporeal shockwave therapy a secondary option, and isolated non-steroid injections no substantial benefit over placebo. (PMID:38613563)
- Ultrasound is described as essential to achieve the best evidence-based outcomes, because first dorsal compartment anatomy varies and ultrasound detects variants accurately. (PMID:35077418)
- A septum dividing the first compartment and a single slip of abductor pollicis longus were more frequent in De Quervain patients than in normal cadavers. (PMID:27693273)
- A conservative-management review proposes a level I-II evidence treatment algorithm and notes ultrasound-guided injections are more accurate and give better outcomes. (PMID:32590652)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Quality of trials and heterogeneity in immobilisation protocols.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-line treatment: steroid injection plus short thumb spica.
- Why an intracompartmental septum predicts injection failure (anatomical variants).

### Case Generation Hooks
- New mother with radial wrist pain on thumb movement: examination and treatment.

**Reviewer Gap**: Clinical test (Finkelstein), the tendons involved and indications for surgery are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27693273**: *Surgical anatomy of the first extensor compartment: A systematic review and comparison of normal cadavers vs. De Quervain syndrome patients* (2017) [systematic review]
- **PMID 32590652**: *Conservative Management of de Quervain Stenosing Tenosynovitis: Review and Presentation of Treatment Algorithm* (2020) [systematic review]
- **PMID 35077418**: *Utility of Ultrasonography and Significance of Surgical Anatomy in the Management of de Quervain Disease: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2022) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 37889490**: *Management of de Quervain Tenosynovitis: A Systematic Review and Network Meta-Analysis* (2023) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 38613563**: *Advancements in de Quervain Tenosynovitis Management: A Comprehensive Network Meta-Analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis]

## [0516] dermatitis

**Cluster**: eczema | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Atopic dermatitis is a chronic inflammatory skin disease managed with emollients and topical anti-inflammatories, escalating to targeted systemic therapy (biologics and JAK inhibitors) for moderate to severe disease. Living guidelines now cover the newest agents.

### Core Teaching Points
- The EuroGuiDerm living guideline covers conventional immunosuppressants (azathioprine, ciclosporin, glucocorticosteroids, methotrexate, mycophenolate), biologics (dupilumab, lebrikizumab, nemolizumab, tralokinumab) and JAK inhibitors (abrocitinib, baricitinib, upadacitinib), with attention to paediatric, adolescent and pregnant patients. (PMID:40317496)
- The 2025 AAD focused update made strong recommendations for tapinarof cream, roflumilast cream, lebrikizumab and nemolizumab with concomitant topical therapy in adults. (PMID:40531067)
- Most trials of atopic dermatitis therapy are short, which limits conclusions about long-term comparative efficacy and safety. (PMID:40531067)
- The German-language S3 guideline Part 2 covers systemic treatment, including dupilumab, tralokinumab, JAK inhibitors, ciclosporin and systemic glucocorticoids. (PMID:38161245)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term comparative safety of biologics versus JAK inhibitors.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Which classes of systemic therapy exist for moderate to severe atopic dermatitis.
- Topical non-steroid options (tapinarof, roflumilast) with strong recommendation in adults.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Adult with severe flexural eczema failing topical steroids and emollients: next step?

**Reviewer Gap**: The existing 'dermatitis' and 'eczema' DB topics are generic ai_generated entries; contact and seborrhoeic dermatitis are not covered here.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38161245**: *S3 guideline Atopic dermatitis: Part 2 - Systemic treatment* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 40317496**: *European Guideline (EuroGuiDerm) on atopic eczema: Living update* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40531067**: *Focused update: Guidelines of care for the management of atopic dermatitis in adults* (2025) [guideline]

## [0517] Dermatomyositis and Antisynthetase Syndrome

**Cluster**: dermatomyositis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Dermatomyositis and antisynthetase syndrome are idiopathic inflammatory myopathies with skin, muscle and often lung involvement. Anti-MDA5 disease with interstitial lung disease is the high-mortality subset.

### Core Teaching Points
- In anti-MDA5-positive dermatomyositis with interstitial lung disease, older age, smoking, fever, raised inflammatory markers and rapidly progressive ILD were mortality risk factors. (PMID:40746563)
- IVIg probably improves disability and muscle strength versus placebo in refractory idiopathic inflammatory myopathy (moderate-certainty); trial risk of bias was mostly high or unclear. (PMID:40787733)
- A single-arm meta-analysis suggests JAK inhibitors may help dermatomyositis and polymyositis without severe adverse reactions, though evidence is limited. (PMID:38576610)
- IMACS published consensus nomenclature and abbreviations for anti-synthetase syndrome. (PMID:40286313)
- The PReS/CARRA treat-to-target recommendations for juvenile dermatomyositis keep high-dose glucocorticoids as the initial therapy with tapering and discontinuation within 12 months; this is paediatric guidance. (PMID:40410052)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Evidence for adult dermatomyositis treatment is largely low-certainty.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Anti-MDA5 antibody and rapidly progressive ILD prognosis.
- Role of IVIg in refractory myositis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Woman with heliotrope rash, mechanic's hands, dyspnoea and rapidly worsening hypoxia: which antibody is concerning?

**Reviewer Gap**: Adult diagnostic criteria, cancer screening and antisynthetase antibody specificity are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38576610**: *Therapeutic efficacy and safety of JAK inhibitors in treating polymyositis/dermatomyositis: a single-arm systemic meta-analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40286313**: *Consensus nomenclature and abbreviation for anti-synthetase syndrome: an IMACS project* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40410052**: *Treating juvenile dermatomyositis to target: Paediatric Rheumatology European Society/Childhood Arthritis and Rheumatology Research Alliance-endorsed recommendations from an international task force* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40746563**: *Risk factors for mortality in anti-MDA5 antibody-positive dermatomyositis with interstitial lung disease: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40787733**: *Non-targeted immunosuppressive and immunomodulatory therapies for idiopathic inflammatory myopathies* (2025) [meta-analysis]

## [0518] device-related infections

**Cluster**: device_infections | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Device and catheter-related infections are largely preventable. Prevention bundles, ultrasound-guided insertion and antiseptic barrier caps lower risk, and paired cultures help decide whether a line must come out.

### Core Teaching Points
- Ultrasound-guided central venous catheter insertion significantly reduced catheter-related infection compared with landmark technique (pooled RR about 0.65), especially in experienced operators. (PMID:39563416)
- Antiseptic barrier caps were associated with fewer central-line bloodstream infections (RR 0.65), though most studies were of poor methodological quality. (PMID:36116679)
- Differential time to positivity (catheter culture growing at least 2 hours before peripheral) performs well for ruling CLABSI in or out, but is less reliable for Staphylococcus aureus (low sensitivity) and Candida (low specificity). (PMID:37062596)
- The 2024 ISID position paper consolidates evidence-based strategies for preventing vascular catheter infections in both high- and low-resource settings. (PMID:39505252)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The DB topic title is 'device-related infections', which is broader than catheters (pacemakers, prosthetic joints); only catheter evidence was retrieved.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Interpreting differential time to positivity.
- Insertion and maintenance measures that reduce CLABSI.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Febrile patient with a tunnelled line: paired cultures and decision about line removal.

**Reviewer Gap**: Cardiac implantable device and prosthetic joint infection are not covered; consider splitting this topic.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36116679**: *Antiseptic barrier caps to prevent central line-associated bloodstream infections: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2023) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 37062596**: *Utility of Differential Time to Positivity in Diagnosing Central Line-Associated Bloodstream Infections: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2023) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 39505252**: *Preventing central line-associated bloodstream infections: A position paper of the International Society for Infectious Diseases, 2024 update* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 39563416**: *Central venous catheter-related infections: a systematic review, meta-analysis, trial sequential analysis and meta-regression comparing ultrasound guidance and landmark technique for insertion* (2024) [meta-analysis]

## [0519] dexamethasone COVID-19 hospitalized mechanical ventilation mortality

**Cluster**: dexamethasone_covid | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Dexamethasone reduced mortality in COVID-19 patients needing oxygen or ventilation in RECOVERY, and later syntheses support corticosteroids in severe disease, with no benefit shown in mild cases.

### Core Teaching Points
- RECOVERY showed dexamethasone reduced death by about 35% in ventilated patients and 20% in those on supplemental oxygen, with no benefit in mild cases. (PMID:32610262)
- A 2020 meta-analysis of RCTs and observational studies confirmed a beneficial effect of corticosteroids on short-term mortality and need for mechanical ventilation, with a possible signal of delayed viral clearance and more secondary infections. (PMID:33317589)
- The ERS living-guideline evidence review supported corticosteroids, particularly dexamethasone, and anti-IL-6 receptor antibody therapy in hospitalised patients. (PMID:34911695)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Secondary infection and delayed viral clearance signals were based on sparse data.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Which COVID-19 patients benefit from dexamethasone (oxygen or ventilation) and who does not (mild).
- Other therapy with mortality benefit: anti-IL-6 receptor antibodies.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Admitted patient on 4 L oxygen at day 8 of illness: indication for dexamethasone?

**Reviewer Gap**: The dose used in RECOVERY is not stated in the retrieved abstracts; confirm from the trial paper.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32610262**: *Role of corticosteroid in the management of COVID-19: A systemic review and a Clinician's perspective* (2020) [systematic review]
- **PMID 33317589**: *Corticosteroid use in COVID-19 patients: a systematic review and meta-analysis on clinical outcomes* (2020) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 34911695**: *The impact of therapeutics on mortality in hospitalised patients with COVID-19: systematic review and meta-analyses informing the European Respiratory Society living guideline* (2021) [guideline]

## [0520] Diabetes insipidus: central vs nephrogenic, water deprivation test, desmopressin

**Cluster**: diabetes_insipidus | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Diabetes insipidus is either vasopressin deficiency (central) or resistance (nephrogenic). Copeptin-based testing is replacing the water deprivation test in many settings, and post-pituitary-surgery cases are usually transient.

### Core Teaching Points
- Congenital nephrogenic DI is caused by distal nephron insensitivity to arginine vasopressin; an international expert consensus statement now guides diagnosis and management. (PMID:39438674)
- After transsphenoidal surgery for pituitary adenoma permanent vasopressin deficiency was uncommon (about 2%), but prevalence reached about 30% with craniopharyngioma and 14% with Rathke cleft cyst; diagnostic criteria for post-operative DI vary. (PMID:38996052)
- In children with polyuria-polydipsia syndrome, stimulated copeptin showed pooled sensitivity about 0.98 and specificity about 0.95, offering a less burdensome alternative to the water deprivation test. (PMID:39409072)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Standard protocols for copeptin testing are not yet established, particularly in children.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Central versus nephrogenic DI: response to desmopressin.
- Transient versus permanent DI after pituitary surgery by tumour type.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Polyuria and hypernatraemia on day 2 after transsphenoidal surgery.

**Reviewer Gap**: The water deprivation test steps and adult copeptin cut-offs are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38996052**: *Central diabetes insipidus (vasopressin deficiency) after surgery for pituitary tumours: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 39409072**: *Diagnostic Utility of Copeptin in Pediatric Patients with Polyuria-Polydipsia Syndrome: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 39438674**: *International expert consensus statement on the diagnosis and management of congenital nephrogenic diabetes insipidus (arginine vasopressin resistance)* (2025) [guideline]

---

## [0522] Diabetes prevention lifestyle and metformin

**Cluster**: diabetes_prevention | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Lifestyle change prevents type 2 diabetes mainly in people with impaired glucose tolerance rather than isolated impaired fasting glucose, and metformin is an option where lifestyle is insufficient.

### Core Teaching Points
- In an individual participant data meta-analysis, lifestyle interventions reduced diabetes incidence in impaired glucose tolerance (HR 0.65) and combined IFG plus IGT (HR 0.51) but not isolated impaired fasting glucose (HR 0.97). (PMID:37650824)
- Exercise, metformin and their combination all improved glucose metabolism; exercise appeared superior to metformin in prediabetes whereas metformin appeared superior to exercise in established type 2 diabetes. (PMID:39242178)
- Lifestyle interventions reduced type 2 diabetes incidence and promoted reversion to normoglycaemia in prediabetes, but the effectiveness of digital health interventions has not been sufficiently proven. (PMID:39879607)
- Risk factors for progression from prediabetes to type 2 diabetes include a high fatty liver index; moderate physical activity was protective. (PMID:40165126)
- A 2025 American College of Lifestyle Medicine guideline gives explicit strategies for lifestyle behaviour change for treatment and remission of type 2 diabetes and prediabetes. (PMID:40546763)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal diagnostic criteria for prediabetes and their link to risk of progression.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Which prediabetes phenotype benefits from lifestyle intervention (IGT, not isolated IFG).
- Exercise versus metformin in prediabetes.

### Case Generation Hooks
- BMI 32 adult with HbA1c 6.1%: lifestyle programme versus metformin.

**Reviewer Gap**: Diabetes Prevention Program numbers and weight-loss targets are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37650824**: *Effect of Conventional Lifestyle Interventions on Type 2 Diabetes Incidence by Glucose-Defined Prediabetes Phenotype: An Individual Participant Data Meta-analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials* (2023) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 39242178**: *Effects of exercise, metformin and their combination on glucose metabolism in individuals with abnormal glycaemic control: a systematic review and network meta-analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 39879607**: *Effectiveness of Different Intervention Modes in Lifestyle Intervention for the Prevention of Type 2 Diabetes and the Reversion to Normoglycemia in Adults With Prediabetes: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40165126**: *Risk factors for progression to type 2 diabetes in prediabetes: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40546763**: *Executive Summary of Lifestyle Interventions for Treatment and Remission of Type 2 Diabetes and Prediabetes in Adults: A Clinical Practice Guideline From the American College of Lifestyle Medicine* (2025) [guideline]

## [0523] Diabetic autonomic neuropathy: gastroparesis, orthostatic hypotension, cardiac autonomic neuropathy

**Cluster**: diabetic_autonomic_neuropathy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Diabetic autonomic neuropathy affects cardiovascular, gastrointestinal and other systems. Treatment options for diabetic gastroparesis are limited and trial design problems, especially high placebo response, complicate interpretation.

### Core Teaching Points
- Ghrelin agonists improved nausea, vomiting, early satiety and abdominal pain in diabetic gastroparesis compared with placebo, with similar adverse events. (PMID:31816671)
- Relamorelin showed promise in gastroparesis but headache, dizziness and gastrointestinal symptoms were more frequent in the treated group. (PMID:38058346)
- Placebo response in gastroparesis trials is substantial; future trials should last at least 8 weeks, use validated symptom questionnaires and distinguish gastroparesis subtypes. (PMID:36270614)
- Exercise may help cardiovascular autonomic neuropathy by balancing sympathetic and parasympathetic activity, though most studies were low quality and none examined older patients, poorly controlled diabetes or type 1 diabetes. (PMID:37830705)
- Dietary intervention may promote gastric emptying and glucose stability in diabetic gastroparesis, pending higher-quality trials. (PMID:38957444)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Few licensed options; evidence for acupoint therapies and vitamin B12 combinations is low quality.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Why gastroparesis trials show large placebo responses.
- Role of glycaemic control and diet in diabetic gastroparesis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Long-standing type 1 diabetes with postprandial fullness, vomiting and erratic glucose.

**Reviewer Gap**: Orthostatic hypotension, resting tachycardia, diagnostic autonomic testing and prokinetic drug safety are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31816671**: *Efficacy and Safety of Ghrelin Agonists in Patients with Diabetic Gastroparesis: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2020) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 36270614**: *Response and Adverse Event Rates With Placebo in Gastroparesis: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis* (2023) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 37830705**: *The Effect of Exercise on Cardiovascular Autonomic Nervous Function in Patients with Diabetes: A Systematic Review* (2023) [systematic review]
- **PMID 38058346**: *Relamorelin in Gastroparesis and Diabetic Gastroparesis: A Meta-Analysis on Its Efficacy and Safety* (2023) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 38957444**: *The role of diet in diabetes gastroparesis treatment: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis]

## [0524] Diabetic foot infection and osteomyelitis

**Cluster**: diabetic_foot | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Diabetic foot disease needs multidisciplinary care: offloading is central to ulcer healing and recurrence prevention, and imaging and infection assessment guide osteomyelitis diagnosis.

### Core Teaching Points
- The IWGDF 2023 offloading guideline summarises its recommendations in a clinical pathway aimed at reducing infection, hospitalisation and amputation in people with diabetes-related foot ulcers. (PMID:37226568)
- After ulcer closure, reloading should include maximal offloading, especially in the first 3 months, with loading titrated by a footwear schedule. (PMID:39574416)
- The ACR Appropriateness Criteria (2025 update) address imaging for suspected osteomyelitis of the foot in diabetes. (PMID:41193054)
- A Japanese guideline weakly recommended an imaging procedure for osteomyelitis, negative-pressure wound therapy and platelet-rich plasma in diabetic foot ulcers. (PMID:42802530)
- A Chinese practical guideline covers metabolic control, antithrombotic and anti-infection therapy, debridement, vascular reconstruction, prevention and multidisciplinary tiered care. (PMID:41326029)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Adjunct wound therapies (hyaluronic acid, PRP, NPWT) rest on weak recommendations or small trials.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Why offloading is the key intervention for neuropathic plantar ulcers.
- Post-healing reloading and footwear schedule.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Diabetic with plantar ulcer over the first metatarsal head, probing to bone, raised CRP.

**Reviewer Gap**: Wagner classification, probe-to-bone test, antibiotic choice and duration, and revascularisation criteria are not in the retrieved abstracts; add the IWGDF/IDSA infection guideline.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37226568**: *Guidelines on offloading foot ulcers in persons with diabetes (IWGDF 2023 update)* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 39574416**: *Diabetic Foot Ulcer Beyond Wound Closure: Clinical Practice Guideline* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 41193054**: *ACR Appropriateness Criteria® Suspected Osteomyelitis of the Foot in Patients With Diabetes Mellitus: 2025 Update* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 41326029**: *[Practical guideline on the prevention and management of diabetic foot in China (Ⅰ)]* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 42802530**: *Wound/Pressure Ulcer/Burn Guidelines (2023)-3 Guidelines for the Diagnosis and Treatment of Diabetic Skin Ulcer/Gangrene (3rd Edition)* (2026) [guideline]

## [0526] Diabetic foot: Wagner classification, multidisciplinary care, offloading, osteomyelitis

**Cluster**: diabetic_foot | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Diabetic foot disease needs multidisciplinary care: offloading is central to ulcer healing and recurrence prevention, and imaging and infection assessment guide osteomyelitis diagnosis.

### Core Teaching Points
- The IWGDF 2023 offloading guideline summarises its recommendations in a clinical pathway aimed at reducing infection, hospitalisation and amputation in people with diabetes-related foot ulcers. (PMID:37226568)
- After ulcer closure, reloading should include maximal offloading, especially in the first 3 months, with loading titrated by a footwear schedule. (PMID:39574416)
- The ACR Appropriateness Criteria (2025 update) address imaging for suspected osteomyelitis of the foot in diabetes. (PMID:41193054)
- A Japanese guideline weakly recommended an imaging procedure for osteomyelitis, negative-pressure wound therapy and platelet-rich plasma in diabetic foot ulcers. (PMID:42802530)
- A Chinese practical guideline covers metabolic control, antithrombotic and anti-infection therapy, debridement, vascular reconstruction, prevention and multidisciplinary tiered care. (PMID:41326029)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Adjunct wound therapies (hyaluronic acid, PRP, NPWT) rest on weak recommendations or small trials.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Why offloading is the key intervention for neuropathic plantar ulcers.
- Post-healing reloading and footwear schedule.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Diabetic with plantar ulcer over the first metatarsal head, probing to bone, raised CRP.

**Reviewer Gap**: Wagner classification, probe-to-bone test, antibiotic choice and duration, and revascularisation criteria are not in the retrieved abstracts; add the IWGDF/IDSA infection guideline.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37226568**: *Guidelines on offloading foot ulcers in persons with diabetes (IWGDF 2023 update)* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 39574416**: *Diabetic Foot Ulcer Beyond Wound Closure: Clinical Practice Guideline* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 41193054**: *ACR Appropriateness Criteria® Suspected Osteomyelitis of the Foot in Patients With Diabetes Mellitus: 2025 Update* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 41326029**: *[Practical guideline on the prevention and management of diabetic foot in China (Ⅰ)]* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 42802530**: *Wound/Pressure Ulcer/Burn Guidelines (2023)-3 Guidelines for the Diagnosis and Treatment of Diabetic Skin Ulcer/Gangrene (3rd Edition)* (2026) [guideline]

## [0527] diabetic ketoacidosis

**Cluster**: dka | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Diabetic ketoacidosis and the hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state are managed by a joint 2024 international consensus that updated diagnostic and resolution criteria for the first time in 15 years. SGLT2 inhibitors add a euglycaemic presentation to recognise.

### Core Teaching Points
- The June 2024 consensus report (ADA and partner societies) revised the criteria for diagnosis and resolution of DKA and HHS and gave new recommendations on assessment, management and prevention; the previous version was 15 years old. (PMID:40032308)
- Important controversies in DKA management remain unresolved because high-quality evidence is lacking. (PMID:40257014)
- DKA in SGLT2-inhibitor users presented with lower glucose, HbA1c, creatinine and lactate than DKA in non-users, with no difference in hospital stay, ICU admission or in-hospital mortality. (PMID:40692313)
- Perioperative SGLT2-inhibitor guidance includes monitoring and prevention strategies for euglycaemic diabetic ketoacidosis. (PMID:42067493)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal fluid type and rate, insulin regimen and potassium strategy are listed as controversial in the review but not specified in the retrieved abstract.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Euglycaemic DKA with SGLT2 inhibitors.
- Updated diagnostic and resolution criteria in the 2024 consensus.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Post-operative patient on empagliflozin with vomiting, normal glucose and anion gap acidosis.

**Reviewer Gap**: The specific protocol (fluid, insulin infusion, potassium thresholds, transition to subcutaneous insulin) and HHS management are not in the retrieved abstracts; topics 528 and 529 need the full consensus report before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40032308**: *Hyperglycemic crises in adults: A look at the 2024 consensus report* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40257014**: *Diabetic Ketoacidosis: Considerations and Residual Controversies in Management After the 2024 ADA, EASD, JBDS, AACE, and DST Joint Consensus* (2026) [guideline]
- **PMID 40692313**: *Clinical features and outcomes of diabetic ketoacidosis in patients using SGLT2 inhibitors: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 42067493**: *Perioperative management of patients taking sodium-glucose cotransporter 2 inhibitors: Society for Perioperative Assessment and Quality Improvement (SPAQI) multidisciplinary consensus statement* (2026) [guideline]

## [0532] Diabetic Nephropathy

**Cluster**: diabetic_nephropathy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Diabetic kidney disease is found by routine eGFR and urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio screening, and outcomes depend on starting and sustaining guideline-directed therapy. Real-world data show the biggest gap is in screening, not prescribing.

### Core Teaching Points
- Early detection with routine eGFR and UACR, followed by timely and sustained guideline-directed medical therapy together with lifestyle change, is described as the most effective strategy to alter the natural history of DKD. (PMID:41717165)
- In a real-world analysis, gaps in guideline-concordant DKD care were driven mainly by screening deficiencies rather than treatment failures, and care varied more by healthcare setting than by patient characteristics. (PMID:42512753)
- A Thai real-world study found real-world implementation of guideline-directed therapy in DKD remains inadequate, with a call for regular review and feedback. (PMID:42311550)
- A UK joint ABCD and UKKA guideline provides practical lipid-management recommendations for adults with DKD. (PMID:38971750)
- A 2026 consensus guidance for DKD includes finerenone alongside lifestyle and dietary measures and stresses affordability and patient education. (PMID:42543958)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The four drug classes and their sequencing are not named in the retrieved abstracts.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Screening for DKD: eGFR plus UACR, repeated in all people with diabetes.
- Why implementation, not drug availability, limits outcomes.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Type 2 diabetic with UACR 120 mg/g and eGFR 55: which therapies to start?

**Reviewer Gap**: UACR stage thresholds, RAS blockade titration, SGLT2 inhibitor and finerenone indications are standard KDIGO/ADA content but are not stated in the abstracts retrieved; add from KDIGO 2022 diabetes in CKD guideline.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38971750**: *Clinical practice guideline for the management of lipids in adults with diabetic kidney disease: abbreviated summary of the Joint Association of British Clinical Diabetologists and UK Kidney Association (ABCD-UKKA) Guideline 2024* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 41717165**: *Guideline-Directed Medical Therapy for Diabetic Kidney Disease: Advances in Primary and Secondary Prevention* (2026) [guideline]
- **PMID 42311550**: *Guideline-Directed Medical Therapies for Diabetic Kidney Disease Among Thai People With Type 2 Diabetes: A Real-World Data Based on Theptarin Diabetes Staging* (2026) [guideline]
- **PMID 42512753**: *Gaps in Guideline-Concordant Care in Diabetic Kidney Disease: A Real-World Analysis of Screening, Treatment, and Associated Factors* (2026) [guideline]
- **PMID 42543958**: *Consensus Guidance for the Diagnosis and Management of Diabetic Kidney Disease: Executive Summary* (2026) [guideline]

## [0534] Diabetic peripheral neuropathy: screening, painful neuropathy pharmacotherapy

**Cluster**: diabetic_neuropathy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Painful diabetic peripheral neuropathy is common and under-detected. Risk rises with diabetes duration and nephropathy, systematic screening is advised, and a few non-drug options show benefit in refractory pain.

### Core Teaching Points
- Risk factors for painful diabetic neuropathy include longer diabetes duration (OR 1.05) and nephropathy (OR 1.32); the authors call for targeted screening and standardised diagnostic tools. (PMID:40107621)
- A broader meta-analysis lists retinopathy, nephropathy, cardiovascular disease, hypertension, smoking, alcohol, obesity and age at onset among risk factors for painful diabetic peripheral neuropathy. (PMID:40433609)
- Spinal cord stimulation produced significant pain reduction and quality-of-life gains in painful diabetic neuropathy and outperformed conventional medical therapy. (PMID:40057880)
- Vitamin D supplementation may reduce short-term pain, but small, low-quality trials mean routine testing or supplementation cannot yet be recommended. (PMID:41139799)
- Patients with diabetic peripheral neuropathy and type 2 diabetes had lower lower-limb muscle strength than those without neuropathy. (PMID:40913437)
- A GCC Delphi consensus recommends systematic screening, early multimodal management and better access to guideline-recommended therapies. (PMID:42557853)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Acupuncture may reduce pain versus sham but evidence quality is limited.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Risk factors for painful diabetic neuropathy.
- Role of spinal cord stimulation for refractory pain.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Burning feet at night in a 10-year type 2 diabetic: screening and first-line analgesia.

**Reviewer Gap**: First-line drug classes (duloxetine, pregabalin/gabapentin, tricyclics), monofilament screening and foot-care advice are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40057880**: *Assessing the Efficacy of Spinal Cord Stimulation in Managing Painful Diabetic Neuropathy: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40107621**: *Prevalence and risk factors of painful diabetic neuropathy: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40433609**: *Prevalence and risk factors for painful diabetic peripheral neuropathy: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40913437**: *Association between diabetic peripheral neuropathy and lower limb muscle strength in patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 41139799**: *Vitamin D for Painful Diabetic Neuropathy: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomised Controlled Trials* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 42557853**: *Bridging gaps in diabetic neuropathy care: A Delphi-based regional consensus from the Gulf Cooperation Council* (2026) [guideline]

## [0535] diabetic retinopathy anti vegf therapy

**Cluster**: diabetic_retinopathy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
For vision-threatening diabetic retinopathy and diabetic macular oedema, intravitreal anti-VEGF therapy is now the first-line option over panretinal photocoagulation, with steroid implants, laser and newer agents used for non-responders.

### Core Teaching Points
- In a network meta-analysis anti-VEGF therapy was more effective than panretinal photocoagulation for visual acuity, macular thickness and neovascularisation with a better safety profile, supporting anti-VEGF as first-line for vision-threatening DR. (PMID:40701389)
- Treatment beyond metabolic control comprises intravitreal anti-VEGF or corticosteroids, laser photocoagulation and vitreous surgery; screening is essential because early disease is often asymptomatic. (PMID:40343680)
- For DME, anti-VEGF monotherapy and anti-VEGF plus laser or intravitreal steroid had comparable effects; adding intravitreal steroid gave no significant benefit. (PMID:39973301)
- Switching to faricimab for persistent DME after prior anti-VEGF produced anatomical and functional improvement and lengthened treatment intervals by about 1.5 weeks. (PMID:40571775)
- An early switch to intravitreal dexamethasone implant after failed anti-VEGF tended to give better outcomes than a late switch, though heterogeneity limits the evidence. (PMID:40451142)
- Deep-learning fundus photograph screening showed high discriminative performance in prospective real-world settings. (PMID:41259706)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Best combination or switching strategy in treatment-resistant DME.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-line therapy for centre-involving DME or high-risk PDR: anti-VEGF.
- What to do when DME persists despite anti-VEGF (switch agent or steroid implant).

### Case Generation Hooks
- Type 2 diabetic with blurred central vision and macular thickening on OCT.

**Reviewer Gap**: Screening intervals, retinopathy grading and the role of systemic glycaemic and blood pressure control are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39973301**: *Anti-VEGF monotherapy versus anti-VEGF therapy combined with laser or intravitreal glucocorticoid therapy for diabetic macular edema: A Bayesian network meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40343680**: *Diabetic retinopathy-recommendations for screening and treatment* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40451142**: *Early versus Late Switch to an Intravitreal Dexamethasone Implant after Failed Anti-VEGF Therapy for Diabetic Macular Edema: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40571775**: *Switching to faricimab after prior anti-vascular endothelial growth factor therapy for persistent diabetic macular edema: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40701389**: *Which treatment modality offers the best outcomes for diabetic retinopathy? A systematic review and network meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 41259706**: *Real-World Prospective Validation and Economic Evaluation of Deep Learning- Based Diabetic Retinopathy Detection From Fundus Photographs: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis* (2026) [meta-analysis]

---

- Deep-learning fundus photograph screening showed high discriminative performance in prospective real-world settings. (PMID:41259706)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Best combination or switching strategy in treatment-resistant DME.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-line therapy for centre-involving DME or high-risk PDR: anti-VEGF.
- What to do when DME persists despite anti-VEGF (switch agent or steroid implant).

### Case Generation Hooks
- Type 2 diabetic with blurred central vision and macular thickening on OCT.

**Reviewer Gap**: Screening intervals, retinopathy grading and the role of systemic glycaemic and blood pressure control are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39973301**: *Anti-VEGF monotherapy versus anti-VEGF therapy combined with laser or intravitreal glucocorticoid therapy for diabetic macular edema: A Bayesian network meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40343680**: *Diabetic retinopathy-recommendations for screening and treatment* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40451142**: *Early versus Late Switch to an Intravitreal Dexamethasone Implant after Failed Anti-VEGF Therapy for Diabetic Macular Edema: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40571775**: *Switching to faricimab after prior anti-vascular endothelial growth factor therapy for persistent diabetic macular edema: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40701389**: *Which treatment modality offers the best outcomes for diabetic retinopathy? A systematic review and network meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 41259706**: *Real-World Prospective Validation and Economic Evaluation of Deep Learning- Based Diabetic Retinopathy Detection From Fundus Photographs: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis* (2026) [meta-analysis]

## [0538] diagnosing appendicitis in children

**Cluster**: appendicitis_children | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute appendicitis in children is diagnosed clinically with imaging support and treated by appendicectomy; non-operative antibiotic management of uncomplicated cases is feasible but fails in a sizeable minority.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2025 WSES Jerusalem Guidelines update evidence-based recommendations for the diagnosis and treatment of acute appendicitis; the Swedish national guidelines cover adults and children, including children and pregnant women. (PMID:41604201, PMID:40203150)
- The 2024 IDSA update includes recommendations for diagnostic imaging of suspected acute appendicitis. (PMID:38963819)
- The SAGES guideline made conditional recommendations favouring operative management of both uncomplicated and complicated appendicitis, either immediate or delayed (more than 12 hours). (PMID:38740595)
- In children with simple appendicitis, non-operative management was safe with moderate early success, but failure rises over time and about a third eventually have an appendicectomy. (PMID:38158255)
- A 2026 meta-analysis found significantly higher treatment failure and major complication rates within a year with non-operative management in children than earlier studies suggested. (PMID:41046476)
- A meta-analysis of RCTs found non-operative management safe and feasible, with success comparable to surgery in children without an appendicolith. (PMID:40133910)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Longer-term failure and complication rates of non-operative management; evidence is conflicting between meta-analyses.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Who is a candidate for non-operative management (uncomplicated, no appendicolith).
- Failure rate of antibiotics-only therapy over time.

### Case Generation Hooks
- 7-year-old with migratory right iliac fossa pain and fever: imaging choice and options for treatment.

**Reviewer Gap**: Clinical scores (Alvarado, PAS), ultrasound-first pathway and perforation management are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38158255**: *Systematic Review and Meta-analysis to Compare the Short- and Long-term Outcomes of Non-operative Management With Early Operative Management of Simple Appendicitis in Children After the COVID-19 Pandemic* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 38740595**: *SAGES guideline for the diagnosis and treatment of appendicitis* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 38963819**: *2024 Clinical Practice Guideline Update by the Infectious Diseases Society of America on Complicated Intra-abdominal Infections: Diagnostic Imaging of Suspected Acute Appendicitis in Adults, Children, and Pregnant People* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 40133910**: *Non-operative management of uncomplicated appendicitis in children, why not? A meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40203150**: *Swedish national guidelines for diagnosis and management of acute appendicitis in adults and children* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 41046476**: *Reevaluating Nonoperative Management for Pediatric Uncomplicated Acute Appendicitis: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2026) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 41604201**: *Diagnosis and Treatment of Acute Appendicitis: 2025 Edition of the World Society of Emergency Surgery Jerusalem Guidelines* (2026) [guideline]

## [0539] diagnosing prediabetes

**Cluster**: prediabetes_dx | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Prediabetes definitions differ between guidelines worldwide, and many people with it are undiagnosed. Identification matters because progression to type 2 diabetes can be reduced.

### Core Teaching Points
- Prediabetes guidelines diverge globally; the 1-hour plasma glucose test is an emerging approach that may improve diagnostic consistency, and a hybrid of universal standards with population-specific adaptation is proposed. (PMID:40157611)
- A large share of people with prediabetes and diabetes in South Asia is undiagnosed, with prevalence rising and higher in urban than rural areas. (PMID:39591894)
- A high fatty liver index is a risk factor for progression from prediabetes to type 2 diabetes, and moderate physical activity is protective. (PMID:40165126)
- Intermittent fasting reduced weight and BMI in prediabetes and type 2 diabetes without effects on glycaemic markers versus continuous energy restriction. (PMID:38956175)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Which diagnostic criteria best predict progression.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Why different guidelines classify the same patient differently.
- Risk factors that predict conversion.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Overweight 45-year-old with fasting glucose in the grey zone and fatty liver on ultrasound.

**Reviewer Gap**: Numeric thresholds (fasting glucose, HbA1c, OGTT) differ by ADA, WHO and IEC and are not in the retrieved abstracts; add from ADA Standards of Care.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38956175**: *The effects of intermittent fasting on body composition and cardiometabolic health in adults with prediabetes or type 2 diabetes: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 39591894**: *Rising trends of diabetes in South Asia: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40157611**: *Divergence in prediabetes guidelines - A global perspective* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40165126**: *Risk factors for progression to type 2 diabetes in prediabetes: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]

## [0540] diagnosing von Willebrand disease

**Cluster**: vwd | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Von Willebrand disease diagnosis and management follow the 2021 ASH/ISTH/NBDF/WFH guideline, which a 2025 monitoring review found still current. Subtypes such as 2B need special care in pregnancy.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2025 monitoring report concluded that none of the newly reviewed studies would change the direction or strength of the 2021 VWD guideline recommendations. (PMID:40273329)
- An ISTH consensus of 25 statements guides care of women with type 2B VWD during pregnancy and postpartum. (PMID:42248413)
- The ISTH retained the name platelet-type VWD (PT-VWD) after a survey failed to reach consensus on renaming. (PMID:40328428)
- Worldwide practice varies in recognising and managing bleeding disorders of unknown cause, and consensus pathways are needed. (PMID:38866246)
- In women with heavy menstrual bleeding, FVIII and FV tended to be slightly higher than in controls across menstrual phases, with VWF activity not significantly different. (PMID:40534379)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Timing of testing relative to the menstrual cycle and acute-phase effects on VWF levels.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Why type 2B VWD needs special monitoring in pregnancy.
- Platelet-type VWD as a mimic of type 2B.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Young woman with heavy menstrual bleeding and easy bruising: first-line tests.

**Reviewer Gap**: Diagnostic algorithm (VWF antigen, activity, FVIII, multimers), desmopressin responsiveness and treatment of bleeds are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38866246**: *Examining variability in the diagnosis and management of people with bleeding disorders of unknown cause: communication from the ISTH SSC Subcommittee on von Willebrand Factor* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 40273329**: *2025 ASH ISTH NBDF WFH monitoring report on the 2021 clinical guidelines on the diagnosis and management of von Willebrand disease* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40328428**: *International opinion survey on nomenclature for platelet-type von Willebrand disease: communication from the SSC of the ISTH* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40534379**: *Clotting Factor Concentration During Menstrual Phases in Women With and Without Heavy Menstrual Bleeding: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 42248413**: *Management of women with type 2B von Willebrand disease during pregnancy and postpartum: guidance from ISTH SSC subcommittees on von Willebrand factor and women's health issues in thrombosis and hemostasis* (2026) [guideline]
