## [0541] diagnosis and management of alcoholic hepatitis

**Cluster**: alcoholic_hepatitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Severe alcohol-associated hepatitis still carries high short-term mortality with limited effective therapy. Prognosis is tracked with MELD, and selected non-responders with low relapse risk may be considered for early liver transplantation.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ACG 2024 alcohol-associated liver disease guideline addresses selected patients with alcohol-associated hepatitis who are unresponsive to medical therapy and at low risk of relapse to alcohol use after transplant. (PMID:38174913)
- Short-term mortality in severe alcohol-associated hepatitis has not improved in recent decades, and MELD is significantly associated with mortality. (PMID:40974371)
- Baclofen is the only intervention with randomised evidence of significant benefit for alcohol use disorder in cirrhosis. (PMID:37602505)
- G-CSF may reduce sepsis and mortality in acute alcoholic hepatitis and decompensated cirrhosis, but results diverge between Asian and European RCTs. (PMID:37716522)
- Faecal microbiota transplantation improved survival in severe alcohol-associated hepatitis at 28 days (RR 2.30) and 90 days (RR 2.53) in pooled data, pending a multicentre RCT. (PMID:40359297)
- People with alcohol-related liver disease have excess non-liver mortality: cardiovascular death RR 2.4, non-hepatic cancer RR 2.2 and infection RR 8.2. (PMID:36694995)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Role of FMT and G-CSF remains investigational.
- Corticosteroid use and the Maddrey/Lille approach are not covered here.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Prognostic scoring in severe alcohol-associated hepatitis (MELD).
- Early liver transplantation in selected non-responders.
- Which AUD drug has RCT evidence in cirrhosis (baclofen).

### Case Generation Hooks
- 45-year-old with jaundice, fever and tender hepatomegaly after weeks of heavy drinking.

**Reviewer Gap**: Diagnostic criteria (NIAAA), corticosteroid indication and response assessment are not in these abstracts; the stored ALD documents from the earlier ingest cover them.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36694995**: *Extra-hepatic morbidity and mortality in alcohol-related liver disease: Systematic review and meta-analysis* (2023) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 37602505**: *Systematic review: Interventions for alcohol use disorder in patients with cirrhosis or alcohol-associated hepatitis* (2023) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 37716522**: *Granulocyte colony stimulating factor in decompensated cirrhosis, acute alcoholic hepatitis, and acute-on-chronic liver failure: A comprehensive meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials* (2023) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 38174913**: *ACG Clinical Guideline: Alcohol-Associated Liver Disease* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 40359297**: *A systematic review and meta-analysis on the efficacy of fecal microbiome transplantation in patients with severe alcohol-associated hepatitis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40974371**: *Meta-Analysis: Mortality Trends and Risk Factors in Severe Alcohol-Associated Hepatitis* (2025) [meta-analysis]

## [0542] diagnosis and management of bacterial meningitis

**Cluster**: bacterial_meningitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Bacterial meningitis is a medical emergency needing prompt empirical antimicrobials with dexamethasone, a low threshold for lumbar puncture and imaging, and attention to raised intracranial pressure. WHO issued its first dedicated meningitis guidelines in 2025.

### Core Teaching Points
- WHO 2025 guidelines provide a unified framework spanning prevention, acute management and long-term care of meningitis. (PMID:40393410, PMID:42019482)
- In low- and middle-income settings, meningitis should be suspected with fever and warrants a low threshold for CSF analysis and neuroimaging; early empirical antimicrobials and dexamethasone are critical, and tuberculous meningitis is the leading cause in endemic regions. (PMID:40831256)
- NICE guidance on bacterial meningitis and meningococcal disease covers recognition, diagnosis and management. (PMID:38843370)
- Strategies to normalise intracranial pressure, including continuous invasive monitoring and CSF diversion, point towards better outcomes in community-acquired bacterial meningitis, with weak evidence certainty. (PMID:38356077)
- Blood biomarkers (IL-6, procalcitonin, CRP, ferritin) had good but not excellent accuracy for paediatric bacterial meningitis. (PMID:39672463)
- Heparin-binding protein showed high diagnostic accuracy across bacterial infections including meningitis, but thresholds are not established. (PMID:38297213)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- When and how to use ICP-directed strategies.
- Optimal biomarker thresholds.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Early empirical antibiotics plus dexamethasone.
- When neuroimaging should precede lumbar puncture.
- Tuberculous meningitis as the leading cause in endemic regions.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Febrile patient with headache, neck stiffness and non-blanching rash: first hour management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific antibiotic regimens by age, dexamethasone timing and CSF interpretation are not in the retrieved abstracts; add from the NICE or WHO full text.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38297213**: *The diagnostic utility of heparin-binding protein among patients with bacterial infections: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 38356077**: *Detection and Management of Elevated Intracranial Pressure in the Treatment of Acute Community-Acquired Bacterial Meningitis: A Systematic Review* (2024) [systematic review]
- **PMID 38843370**: *Meningitis (bacterial) and meningococcal disease: recognition, diagnosis and management* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 39672463**: *Biomarkers in paediatric bacterial meningitis: a systematic review and meta-analysis of diagnostic test accuracy* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40393410**: *WHO guidelines on meningitis diagnosis, treatment and care* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40831256**: *Clinical guidelines for meningitis in low- and middle-income countries* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 42019482**: *Global bacterial meningitis guidelines: from policy to action* (2026) [guideline]

## [0543] Diagnostic Colonoscopy

**Cluster**: colonoscopy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Colonoscopy quality depends on adequate bowel preparation and follow-up of positive screening tests. Computer-aided polyp detection raises adenoma detection but guideline bodies give only weak recommendations because patient-important benefit is uncertain.

### Core Teaching Points
- The US Multi-Society Task Force suggests adjunctive oral simethicone for bowel preparation and recommends tracking the rate of adequate preparations for each endoscopist and unit, with a target above 90%. (PMID:40035345)
- ESGE made a weak recommendation on computer-assisted detection colonoscopy because evidence is limited, absolute benefits for cancer incidence and mortality are small, and burdens include polyp overdiagnosis and more surveillance colonoscopies. (PMID:40148135)
- A living guideline judged the effect of computer-aided detection on colorectal cancer incidence over 10 years highly uncertain and the effect on cancer mortality low-certainty with little to no impact. (PMID:40147837)
- The 2026 American Cancer Society update states that a positive non-colonoscopy screening test needs timely follow-up colonoscopy, preferably within 6 months, to complete screening. (PMID:42200680)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether higher adenoma detection from AI translates into fewer cancers or deaths.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Quality indicator for bowel preparation (adequate in more than 90%).
- Next step after a positive stool-based test (colonoscopy within 6 months).

### Case Generation Hooks
- Positive FIT in a 52-year-old: timing and quality requirements for the follow-up colonoscopy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Indications, surveillance intervals, adenoma detection rate targets, sedation and perforation risk are not in the retrieved abstracts; see the endoscopy quality cluster.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40035345**: *Optimizing Bowel Preparation Quality for Colonoscopy: Consensus Recommendations by the US Multi-Society Task Force on Colorectal Cancer* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40147837**: *Computer aided detection and diagnosis of polyps in adult patients undergoing colonoscopy: a living clinical practice guideline* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40148135**: *Use of computer-assisted detection (CADe) colonoscopy in colorectal cancer screening and surveillance: European Society of Gastrointestinal Endoscopy (ESGE) Position Statement* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 42200680**: *Colorectal cancer screening: An update to the American Cancer Society guideline, 2026* (2026) [guideline]

## [0544] diagnostic test for chronic fatigue syndrome

**Cluster**: cfs | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Myalgic encephalomyelitis/chronic fatigue syndrome is diagnosed clinically; no single laboratory test is established. Recent NICE guidance places graded approaches and CBT in a supportive role only.

### Core Teaching Points
- A systematic review of ME/CFS biomarkers found immune dysfunction studies but heterogeneity across studies and a need for uniform protocols, so no validated diagnostic test is identified. (PMID:37226227)
- EUROMENE, a 22-country expert consensus, gives recommendations for clinical diagnosis, health services and care for people with ME/CFS. (PMID:34069603)
- The 2021 NICE guideline advises that cognitive behavioural therapy is offered only as a supportive intervention, not as a cure. (PMID:36553962)
- About half of people with long COVID meet criteria for ME/CFS, and fibromyalgia and ME/CFS overlap in about 47% of reported cases. (PMID:39353473, PMID:35690247)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Diagnostic criteria differ between bodies and the NICE guideline has been contested.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- ME/CFS is a clinical diagnosis; investigations exclude other causes.
- Post-exertional malaise and why graded exercise is no longer advised.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Patient with 8 months of disabling fatigue, unrefreshing sleep and symptom worsening after exertion following a viral illness.

**Reviewer Gap**: The core symptom criteria (post-exertional malaise, unrefreshing sleep, cognitive impairment) and a screening investigation list are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34069603**: *European Network on Myalgic Encephalomyelitis/Chronic Fatigue Syndrome (EUROMENE): Expert Consensus on the Diagnosis, Service Provision, and Care of People with ME/CFS in Europe* (2021) [guideline]
- **PMID 35690247**: *Clinical overlap between fibromyalgia and myalgic encephalomyelitis. A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2022) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 36553962**: *What Primary Care Practitioners Need to Know about the New NICE Guideline for Myalgic Encephalomyelitis/Chronic Fatigue Syndrome in Adults* (2022) [guideline]
- **PMID 37226227**: *Biomarkers for myalgic encephalomyelitis/chronic fatigue syndrome (ME/CFS): a systematic review* (2023) [systematic review]
- **PMID 39353473**: *The persistence of myalgic encephalomyelitis/chronic fatigue syndrome (ME/CFS) after SARS-CoV-2 infection: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis]

## [0545] Diagnostic Upper GI Endoscopy

**Cluster**: upper_gi_endoscopy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Diagnostic upper GI endoscopy is guided by quality standards that stress careful mucosal inspection, and by urgency rules for conditions such as foreign bodies.

### Core Teaching Points
- The BSG/AUGIS quality standards give 38 recommendations aimed at improving diagnostic endoscopy through mucosal inspection and lesion recognition, to support early diagnosis. (PMID:28821598)
- ESGE recommends urgent (within 24 hours) therapeutic endoscopy for gastric foreign bodies such as sharp-pointed objects, magnets, batteries and large or long objects. (PMID:26862844)
- ESGE recommends earlier endoscopic examination than planned surveillance if a patient with a polyposis syndrome becomes symptomatic. (PMID:31342472)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Indications and sedation are not covered in the retrieved sources.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Which swallowed foreign bodies need endoscopy within 24 hours.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Child who swallowed a button battery and a pair of magnets.

**Reviewer Gap**: Alarm-feature indications, biopsy protocols, complications and consent are not in the retrieved abstracts; this page is thin and needs a general upper GI endoscopy guideline.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26862844**: *Removal of foreign bodies in the upper gastrointestinal tract in adults: European Society of Gastrointestinal Endoscopy (ESGE) Clinical Guideline* (2016) [guideline]
- **PMID 28821598**: *Quality standards in upper gastrointestinal endoscopy: a position statement of the British Society of Gastroenterology (BSG) and Association of Upper Gastrointestinal Surgeons of Great Britain and Ireland (AUGIS)* (2017) [guideline]
- **PMID 31342472**: *Endoscopic management of polyposis syndromes: European Society of Gastrointestinal Endoscopy (ESGE) Guideline* (2019) [guideline]

## [0546] Dialysis initiation timing in advanced CKD

**Cluster**: dialysis_timing | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Starting dialysis early on the basis of eGFR alone has not been shown to improve survival; decisions rest on symptoms, volume status, complications and patient preference.

### Core Teaching Points
- A systematic review found that criteria for starting dialysis vary, most studies showed eGFR at initiation was not associated with mortality, and timing should not be based on GFR alone; volume load and tolerance of overload are more useful. (PMID:37286965)
- A meta-analysis found early start of dialysis increased mortality risk compared with late start (including HR 1.20 in those younger than 65). (PMID:30062691)
- The 2024 JAMA review states that timing and modality should be guided by symptoms, laboratory trajectories, patient preferences and cost and availability, using shared decision-making. (PMID:39356511)
- Unplanned-start peritoneal dialysis carried higher risks of mechanical complications, transition to haemodialysis and possibly mortality, depending on break-in period. (PMID:33120399)
- In elderly haemodialysis patients functional and cognitive impairment and falls were strongly associated with mortality, as was catheter access (OR 1.53). (PMID:32867718)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Observational timing studies are confounded by lead-time and illness severity.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Early versus late dialysis start: no survival benefit from eGFR-triggered early start.
- Indications to start dialysis in practice (uraemic symptoms, refractory volume overload).

### Case Generation Hooks
- Asymptomatic 70-year-old with eGFR 8: start now or wait?

**Reviewer Gap**: IDEAL trial figures and absolute indications (refractory hyperkalaemia, acidosis, pericarditis) are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30062691**: *Timing of Dialysis Initiation and Mortality Risk in Chronic Kidney Disease: A Meta-Analysis* (2018) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 32867718**: *Risk factors for mortality in elderly haemodialysis patients: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2020) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 33120399**: *Outcome and Safety of Unplanned-Start Peritoneal Dialysis according to Break-In Periods: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2021) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 37286965**: *Update of dialysis initiation timing in end stage kidney disease patients: is it a resolved question? A systematic literature review* (2023) [systematic review]
- **PMID 39356511**: *Dialysis for Chronic Kidney Failure: A Review* (2024) [review]

## [0547] Differentiated thyroid cancer: papillary vs follicular, TNM staging, RAI ablation

**Cluster**: dtc | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The 2025 ATA guideline revises management of differentiated thyroid cancer towards de-escalation for low-risk disease, and risk stratification now guides surgery, radioiodine and surveillance.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2025 ATA guideline replaces the 2015 version; changes include de-escalation of surveillance for low-risk patients and the new concept of complete remission. (PMID:41173539, PMID:40844370)
- The 2024 Korean update downgraded some categories involving strap-muscle invasion to lower risk, aiming to reduce overtreatment in low-risk patients while intensifying management for higher-risk disease. (PMID:40598904)
- Radioactive-iodine-refractory disease requires a multidisciplinary, personalised approach, with assessment of comorbidities and toxicity before systemic therapy. (PMID:40345488)
- In the 2015 ATA era, Medicaid-insured patients were less likely to receive guideline-concordant timely surgery and more likely to be undertreated with RAI. (PMID:37141704)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal surveillance intensity in low-risk disease.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Low-risk DTC: de-escalated surgery and surveillance.
- When RAI-refractory disease is considered.

### Case Generation Hooks
- 1.2 cm papillary carcinoma without nodal disease: lobectomy or total thyroidectomy, RAI or not.

**Reviewer Gap**: Histology comparison (papillary versus follicular), TNM staging and TSH targets are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37141704**: *Insurance-Based Disparities in Guideline-Concordant Thyroid Cancer Care in the Era of De-escalation* (2023) [guideline]
- **PMID 40345488**: *ENDOCAN TUTHYREF network consensus recommendations: Refractory follicular-derived thyroid cancer* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40598904**: *The Initial Risk Stratification System for Differentiated Thyroid Cancer: Key Updates in the 2024 Korean Thyroid Association Guideline* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40844370**: *2025 American Thyroid Association Management Guidelines for Adult Patients with Differentiated Thyroid Cancer* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 41173539**: *Executive Summary of the 2025 American Thyroid Association Management Guidelines for Adult Patients with Differentiated Thyroid Cancer* (2025) [guideline]

## [0548] difficult airway

**Cluster**: difficult_airway | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Difficult airway management is organised around structured national guidelines; the 2025 Difficult Airway Society update emphasises maximising the chance of success at each attempt rather than concentrating on failure management.

### Core Teaching Points
- The Difficult Airway Society 2025 guideline on unanticipated difficult tracheal intubation in adults stresses maximising chances of success rather than avoidance and management of failure. (PMID:41203471)
- DAS has separate guidance for airway management in suspected or confirmed cervical spine injury. (PMID:38699880)
- SOBA best-practice recommendations address airway management in patients living with obesity, including intubation, supraglottic devices and extubation, without replacing existing airway guidelines. (PMID:40468902)
- DAS and partners published multidisciplinary guidance on management of haematoma after thyroid surgery, whose principles may apply to other neck surgery. (PMID:34545943)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Specific plan A to D steps are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Special populations: cervical spine injury, obesity, post-thyroidectomy haematoma.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Failed intubation with falling saturations: escalate through the airway algorithm.

**Reviewer Gap**: The stepwise algorithm (facemask, supraglottic airway, front-of-neck access) and videolaryngoscopy evidence are not in the retrieved abstracts; this page needs the DAS full text.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34545943**: *Management of haematoma after thyroid surgery: systematic review and multidisciplinary consensus guidelines from the Difficult Airway Society, the British Association of Endocrine and Thyroid Surgeons and the British Association of Otorhinolaryngology, Head and Neck Surgery* (2022) [guideline]
- **PMID 38699880**: *Airway management in patients with suspected or confirmed cervical spine injury: Guidelines from the Difficult Airway Society (DAS), Association of Anaesthetists (AoA), British Society of Orthopaedic Anaesthetists (BSOA), Intensive Care Society (ICS), Neuro Anaesthesia and Critical Care Society (NACCS), Faculty of Prehospital Care and Royal College of Emergency Medicine (RCEM)* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 40468902**: *Airway management in patients living with obesity: best practice recommendations from the Society for Obesity and Bariatric Anaesthesia: Endorsed by the All Wales Airway Group, Scottish Airway Group and Difficult Airway Society* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 41203471**: *Difficult Airway Society 2025 guidelines for management of unanticipated difficult tracheal intubation in adults* (2026) [guideline]

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Difficult airway management is organised around structured national guidelines; the 2025 Difficult Airway Society update emphasises maximising the chance of success at each attempt rather than concentrating on failure management.

### Core Teaching Points
- The Difficult Airway Society 2025 guideline on unanticipated difficult tracheal intubation in adults stresses maximising chances of success rather than avoidance and management of failure. (PMID:41203471)

- Specific plan A to D steps are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Special populations: cervical spine injury, obesity, post-thyroidectomy haematoma.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Failed intubation with falling saturations: escalate through the airway algorithm.

**Reviewer Gap**: The stepwise algorithm (facemask, supraglottic airway, front-of-neck access) and videolaryngoscopy evidence are not in the retrieved abstracts; this page needs the DAS full text.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 40468902**: *Airway management in patients living with obesity: best practice recommendations from the Society for Obesity and Bariatric Anaesthesia: Endorsed by the All Wales Airway Group, Scottish Airway Group and Difficult Airway Society* (2025) [guideline]

## [0550] Diffuse Alveolar Hemorrhage

**Cluster**: dah | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Diffuse alveolar haemorrhage is a life-threatening pulmonary syndrome of immune or other causes. Management is treatment of the cause with high-dose immunosuppression, with extracorporeal support available in refractory cases.

### Core Teaching Points
- In granulomatosis with polyangiitis presenting with DAH, steroids, rituximab and cyclophosphamide are first-line treatment, plasmapheresis is still used, and ECMO can be salvage therapy. (PMID:36348918)
- In SLE, the need for plasmapheresis and mechanical ventilation marked poor outcome, and antiphospholipid antibody positivity (especially lupus anticoagulant and anticardiolipin IgG) increased DAH risk. (PMID:33593433, PMID:39558618)
- Antiphospholipid-syndrome DAH carries estimated mortality of 30.3% at one year and 45.8% at five years, with worse outcomes with triple positivity, thrombocytopenia, valvular involvement and catastrophic APS. (PMID:37722463)
- Levamisole-adulterated cocaine can cause ANCA-associated pulmonary-renal syndrome; asking about cocaine use is required for diagnosis and counselling. (PMID:37375793)
- In a systematic review of extracorporeal life support for diffuse alveolar damage or haemorrhage, 30-day mortality was 10.5% with no difference between VA and VV ECMO. (PMID:33190331)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- No randomised trials guide treatment of SLE- or APS-associated DAH.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Causes of pulmonary-renal syndrome including cocaine-levamisole vasculitis.
- First-line immunosuppression for ANCA-associated DAH.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Young woman with SLE, haemoptysis, falling haemoglobin and bilateral infiltrates.

**Reviewer Gap**: Bronchoalveolar lavage diagnosis (progressively bloodier aliquots), DLCO increase and steroid dosing are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33190331**: *Utilization of extracorporeal life support for diffuse alveolar damage and diffuse alveolar hemorrhage: A systematic review* (2021) [systematic review]
- **PMID 33593433**: *Risk factors for mortality of diffuse alveolar hemorrhage in systemic lupus erythematosus: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2021) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 36348918**: *Granulomatosis With Polyangiitis Presenting With Diffuse Alveolar Hemorrhage: A Systematic Review* (2022) [systematic review]
- **PMID 37375793**: *Pulmonary-Renal Syndrome from Levamisole-Adulterated Cocaine-Induced Antineutrophil Cytoplasmic Antibody (ANCA)-Associated Vasculitis: A Systematic Review* (2023) [systematic review]
- **PMID 37722463**: *Clinical features, risk factors, and outcomes of diffuse alveolar hemorrhage in antiphospholipid syndrome: A mixed-method approach combining a multicenter cohort with a systematic literature review* (2023) [systematic review]
- **PMID 39558618**: *Association between antiphospholipid antibodies and diffuse alveolar haemorrhage risk in systemic lupus erythematosus: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]

## [0551] Diffuse Idiopathic Skeletal Hyperostosis

**Cluster**: dish | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Diffuse idiopathic skeletal hyperostosis is common in older men, usually incidental, and relevant mainly for dysphagia from cervical osteophytes and for spinal fracture risk.

### Core Teaching Points
- Global prevalence of DISH was about 11.9% in the general population and 14.3% in clinical populations, higher in males and in those aged 50 and over. (PMID:40444238)
- Cervical DISH can cause dysphagia and airway obstruction; surgery is effective with low major complication rates, but randomised studies and guidelines are lacking. (PMID:35283294, PMID:34020047)
- Vertebral fractures are prevalent in both DISH and ankylosing spondylitis, with different distribution along the spine. (PMID:38370336)
- There is no consensus on classification criteria for DISH, and AS and DISH can co-exist and should not be viewed as mutually exclusive. (PMID:28371859, PMID:30060244)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Early-stage definitions and treatment trials.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Dysphagia in an older man with a rigid thoracic spine: cervical osteophytes.
- Spine fracture risk in ankylosing spinal disorders.

### Case Generation Hooks
- 72-year-old man with diabetes, back stiffness and progressive dysphagia.

**Reviewer Gap**: Resnick radiological criteria (flowing ossification over at least four contiguous vertebrae) and sparing of sacroiliac joints are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28371859**: *Classification criteria for diffuse idiopathic skeletal hyperostosis: a lack of consensus* (2017) [systematic review]
- **PMID 30060244**: *Simultaneous occurrence of ankylosing spondylitis and diffuse idiopathic skeletal hyperostosis: a systematic review* (2018) [systematic review]
- **PMID 34020047**: *Otolaryngologic manifestations of diffuse idiopathic skeletal hyperostosis (Forestier's disease): A systematic review of the literature* (2021) [systematic review]
- **PMID 35283294**: *Diffuse idiopathic skeletal hyperostosis of the cervical spine causing dysphagia and airway obstruction: an updated systematic review* (2022) [systematic review]
- **PMID 38370336**: *The prevalence of vertebral fractures in diffuse idiopathic skeletal hyperostosis and ankylosing spondylitis: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40444238**: *Global prevalence estimates of diffuse idiopathic skeletal hyperostosis: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]

## [0552] Diffuse Large B-Cell Lymphoma

**Cluster**: dlbcl | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Relapsed or refractory diffuse large B-cell lymphoma is now treated with CAR T-cell therapy in second line for eligible patients, and CD3xCD20 bispecific antibodies are changing practice in later relapse and are being tested earlier.

### Core Teaching Points
- The British Society for Haematology guideline recommends CAR T-cell therapy or a CD3xCD20 bispecific antibody at second relapse in eligible patients, with specific advice on immunohistochemistry, bendamustine use and bridging to CAR T. (PMID:40384597)
- NCCN 2025 insights report that adding CD3xCD20 bispecific antibodies to chemoimmunotherapy improves outcomes in relapsed/refractory DLBCL. (PMID:41067277)
- A meta-analysis of second-line anti-CD19 CAR T-cell therapy found higher response rates and delayed progression versus standard of care, with progression-free survival benefit for liso-cel (HR 0.37) and axi-cel (HR 0.42) compared with tisa-cel. (PMID:39091918)
- PET/CT is proposed as a central biomarker for early identification of high-risk patients so that CAR T referral is not delayed. (PMID:42360330)
- The SEOM-GOTEL 2025 guideline notes bispecifics may challenge R-CHOP as the benchmark for newly diagnosed DLBCL. (PMID:41129029)
- After CAR T failure, treatment choice depends on performance status, whether disease is localised or systemic and prior therapy; loncastuximab tesirine showed meaningful activity, including post-CAR T, with frequent but manageable cytopenias. (PMID:40849006, PMID:42487493)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Sequencing of CAR T and bispecifics; heterogeneity of real-world loncastuximab data.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Second-line therapy in early-relapsing DLBCL: CAR T versus autologous transplant.
- Role of bispecific antibodies after CAR T.

### Case Generation Hooks
- 62-year-old with DLBCL relapsing 8 months after R-CHOP: next step and role of PET.

**Reviewer Gap**: Frontline R-CHOP and Pola-R-CHP data, prognostic indices (IPI), CAR T toxicities (CRS, ICANS) are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39091918**: *A systematic review and meta-analysis on utilizing anti-CD19 chimeric antigen receptor T-cell therapy as a second-line treatment for relapsed and refractory diffuse large B-cell lymphoma* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40384597**: *Management of relapsed or refractory large B-cell lymphoma: A British Society for Haematology Guideline* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40849006**: *Treatment of Diffuse Large B-cell Lymphoma Progressing or Relapsing after Chimeric Antigen Receptor T-cell Therapy* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 41067277**: *NCCN Guidelines® Insights: B-Cell Lymphomas 3.2025* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 41129029**: *SEOM-GOTEL clinical guidelines on diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (update 2025)* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 42360330**: *PET-guided early identification of CAR-T candidates in diffuse large b-cell lymphoma: a multidisciplinary expert consensus* (2026) [guideline]
- **PMID 42487493**: *Outcomes of loncastuximab tesirine in heavily pretreated patients with diffuse large B-cell lymphoma, including the post-CAR T-cell therapy setting: A meta-analysis* (2026) [meta-analysis]

## [0555] Diffusing Capacity of the Lung for Carbon Monoxide

**Cluster**: dlco | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
DLCO measures gas transfer across the alveolar-capillary membrane and must be interpreted with spirometry, lung volumes and clinical context, taking test variability into account. It tracks severity and prognosis in COPD and recovery after lung injury.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ERS/ATS technical standard states that interpretation of lung function tests must be complemented by clinical expertise and consideration of the test's biological variability and uncertainty. (PMID:34949706)
- In COPD, DLCO relates to disease severity, exacerbation risk, mortality, emphysema predominance and presence of pulmonary hypertension, because it reflects ventilation and perfusion together. (PMID:34855516)
- Four months after critical COVID-19, reduced DLCO was the main finding; risk rose with age over 60, mechanical ventilation and longer ICU stay. (PMID:33901787)
- Lung volume reduction in emphysema improved DLCO by a mean of 5.7% predicted, with no real change in blood gases, plausibly through better V/Q matching. (PMID:33115787)
- DLCO and DLCO/VA are heritable traits, and a functional variant in ADGRG6 was associated with DLCO/VA. (PMID:30049742)
- The ATS recommends standardised formats for pulmonary function reports, including test quality assessment. (PMID:29192835)
- A chest-medicine review outlines a clinically friendly approach to DLCO interpretation and the scenarios where it helps most. (PMID:31078210)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The abstracts do not list conditions with high DLCO or the haemoglobin correction.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Low DLCO in emphysema and interstitial disease with preserved or restrictive spirometry.
- DLCO follow-up after severe COVID-19.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Breathless smoker with obstructive spirometry and markedly low DLCO.

**Reviewer Gap**: Conditions causing raised DLCO (alveolar haemorrhage, polycythaemia), anaemia and carboxyhaemoglobin corrections, and KCO interpretation are standard teaching but not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29192835**: *Recommendations for a Standardized Pulmonary Function Report. An Official American Thoracic Society Technical Statement* (2017) [guideline]
- **PMID 30049742**: *Heritability and genome-wide association study of diffusing capacity of the lung* (2018) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 31078210**: *Incorporating Lung Diffusing Capacity for Carbon Monoxide in Clinical Decision Making in Chest Medicine* (2019) [review]
- **PMID 33115787**: *The effects of lung volume reduction treatment on diffusing capacity and gas exchange* (2020) [systematic review]
- **PMID 33901787**: *Impaired diffusing capacity for carbon monoxide is common in critically ill Covid-19 patients at four months post-discharge* (2021) [review]
- **PMID 34855516**: *Diffusing capacity in chronic obstructive pulmonary disease assessment: A meta-analysis* (2021) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 34949706**: *ERS/ATS technical standard on interpretive strategies for routine lung function tests* (2022) [review]

## [0556] Dilated cardiomyopathy: aetiology workup, genetic testing, device therapy

**Cluster**: dilated_cardiomyopathy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Dilated cardiomyopathy needs aetiological work-up, family screening and genetic assessment alongside guideline-directed heart failure therapy; guidance gaps remain in family screening imaging and aetiology-specific treatment.

### Core Teaching Points
- A systematic review of DCM guidelines identified gaps including use of cardiac MRI in phenotype-negative relatives of genotype-positive patients and development of aetiology-oriented therapies. (PMID:39674807)
- Clinical care of family members of DCM patients is moving to integrate genotype-phenotype knowledge, with open questions in risk prediction and early detection. (PMID:40902100)
- In genotype-positive DCM there is a persistent male excess even though genetic risk is equally prevalent, suggesting additional drivers of sex-biased penetrance. (PMID:39895490)
- Inflammatory cardiomyopathy requires lifelong follow-up because of persistence, arrhythmic burden and recurrence of inflammatory hot phases. (PMID:41747776)
- Pathogenic cardiomyopathy gene variants in atrial fibrillation were associated with higher risk of heart failure hospitalisation and cardiovascular death but not stroke. (PMID:40903137)
- Suboptimal GDMT titration in chronic heart failure was linked to older age and comorbidities, suggesting perceived frailty affects prescribing. (PMID:41136931)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal genetic testing strategy and ICD thresholds are not addressed in the abstracts.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Family screening in DCM and role of genetic testing.
- Titin and other genes associated with arrhythmic risk (not covered here).

### Case Generation Hooks
- 35-year-old with LVEF 30% and a father with sudden death: work-up and family screening.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific aetiology work-up list, device therapy criteria and recommended genes are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39674807**: *Diagnosis and management of dilated cardiomyopathy: a systematic review of clinical practice guidelines and recommendations* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 39895490**: *Systematic Review, Meta-Analysis, and Population Study to Determine the Biologic Sex Ratio in Dilated Cardiomyopathy* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40902100**: *Clinical care of family members of patients with dilated cardiomyopathy* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40903137**: *Pathogenic Cardiomyopathy-Associated Gene Variants and Prognosis in Atrial Fibrillation: Results in 18,000 Clinical Trial Participants* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 41136931**: *Evaluation of prescribing adherence to guideline-directed medical therapy in patients with chronic heart failure: a retrospective study at the National Heart Centre in Oman* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 41747776**: *Inflammatory cardiomyopathy: Position paper of the Italian Society of Cardiology Working Group on cardiomyopathies and pericardial diseases in collaboration with the Italian Society of Cardiology Working Group on cardiac magnetic resonance* (2026) [guideline]

## [0557] Direct oral anticoagulants: dabigatran, rivaroxaban, apixaban, reversal agents

**Cluster**: doacs | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Direct oral anticoagulants are used without routine monitoring; the decisions that matter are perioperative interruption without bridging, specific reversal in life-threatening bleeding, and safe testing of drug levels.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2025 DOAC guidance states DOACs can be interrupted and resumed for procedures without bridging with LMWH, and names specific reversal agents for factor Xa inhibitors and idarucizumab for dabigatran, with prothrombin complex concentrates if these are unavailable. (PMID:40448969)
- The ISTH guidance reviews specific and non-specific reversal agents and states when reversal is indicated. (PMID:39029742)
- A clinical guideline on DOAC reversal in life-threatening bleeding could make no recommendation for recombinant factor VIIa as a non-specific haemostatic agent because of scant data. (PMID:38567679)
- A DOAC dipstick algorithm allows rapid exclusion of clinically relevant drug levels and distinguishes factor Xa from thrombin inhibitors where quantitative assays are unavailable. (PMID:38316416)
- The ACCP perioperative guideline notes that uncertainty remains about best practice for most perioperative antithrombotic questions. (PMID:35964704)
- In cirrhosis, anticoagulation is recommended for symptomatic portal vein thrombosis and suggested for asymptomatic progressing thrombosis. (PMID:38823454)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Evidence for non-specific haemostatic agents is limited.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Reversal agent for dabigatran (idarucizumab) versus factor Xa inhibitors.
- Why bridging is not needed for DOAC interruption.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Patient on apixaban with intracranial haemorrhage: reversal options.

**Reviewer Gap**: Dosing, renal adjustment and drug-drug interactions for each DOAC are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35964704**: *Perioperative Management of Antithrombotic Therapy: An American College of Chest Physicians Clinical Practice Guideline* (2022) [guideline]
- **PMID 38316416**: *Algorithm for Rapid Exclusion of Clinically Relevant Plasma Levels of Direct Oral Anticoagulants in Patients Using the DOAC Dipstick: An Expert Consensus Paper* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 38567679**: *Clinical guideline on reversal of direct oral anticoagulants in patients with life threatening bleeding* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 38823454**: *Anticoagulation for stroke prevention in atrial fibrillation and treatment of venous thromboembolism and portal vein thrombosis in cirrhosis: guidance from the SSC of the ISTH* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 39029742**: *Reversal of direct oral anticoagulants: guidance from the SSC of the ISTH* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 40448969**: *2025 Guidelines for direct oral anticoagulants: a practical guidance on the prescription, laboratory testing, peri-operative and bleeding management* (2025) [guideline]

## [0558] Disseminated Intravascular Coagulation

**Cluster**: dic | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Disseminated intravascular coagulation mortality is very high but varies by underlying disease and diagnostic criteria. Recent Japanese guidelines provide disease-specific recommendations and weak recommendations for certain therapies.

### Core Teaching Points
- A systematic review found DIC mortality very high but heterogeneous depending on underlying disease and diagnostic criteria, with no clear yearly improvement. (PMID:40383152)
- In haematologic malignancy, recombinant thrombomodulin is weakly recommended (Grade 2B), while heparin and serine protease inhibitors are weakly not recommended (Grade 2C). (PMID:39674834)
- For sepsis-associated DIC, the Japanese guidelines made no recommendation on heparin or serine protease inhibitors because of lack of evidence, and listed combination use of antithrombin and recombinant thrombomodulin as a research question. (PMID:39676120)
- For trauma, burn and obstetric DIC, the guidelines provide stratified recommendations including fibrinogen, antithrombin and tranexamic acid. (PMID:39890756)
- Global surveys show major gaps between recommended and real-world DIC practice, especially in resource-limited settings. (PMID:41655787)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether DIC is present and clinically relevant in cirrhosis remains unresolved.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Treat the underlying cause as the cornerstone of DIC management.
- Disease-specific differences in anticoagulant benefit.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Septic patient with falling platelets, prolonged PT and raised D-dimer.

**Reviewer Gap**: ISTH scoring system components and transfusion thresholds are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39674834**: *Clinical practice guidelines for management of disseminated intravascular coagulation in Japan 2024. Part 2: hematologic malignancy* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 39676120**: *Clinical practice guidelines for management of disseminated intravascular coagulation in Japan 2024. Part 1: sepsis* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 39890756**: *Clinical practice guidelines for management of disseminated intravascular coagulation in Japan 2024: part 4-trauma, burn, obstetrics, acute pancreatitis/liver failure, and others* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40383152**: *Mortality, diagnosis, and etiology of disseminated intravascular coagulation-a systematic review and meta-analysis: communication from the ISTH SSC subcommittee on disseminated intravascular coagulation* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 41655787**: *Global practice and challenges in the diagnosis and management of disseminated intravascular coagulation: communication from the ISTH SSC Subcommittee on Disseminated Intravascular Coagulation* (2026) [guideline]

## [0560] Distributive shock: vasoplegic and anaphylactic shock

**Cluster**: distributive_shock | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Distributive shock is managed with fluids and norepinephrine, with vasopressin as the usual second agent and angiotensin II as an option in refractory cases; anaphylactic shock is a distinct adrenaline-dependent form.

### Core Teaching Points
- The Canadian Critical Care Society suggests vasopressin or analogues added to catecholamines over catecholamines alone for distributive shock (conditional recommendation, low-certainty evidence). (PMID:31797234)
- A network meta-analysis in septic shock found norepinephrine plus dobutamine associated with lower 28-day mortality and dopamine with higher mortality than norepinephrine, terlipressin or vasopressin. (PMID:37548686)
- Angiotensin II allowed significant reduction of adjunctive vasopressors at 3 hours without increased adverse events, and expert consensus calls for more trials in refractory vasodilatory shock. (PMID:38888542, PMID:39152516)
- A meta-analysis of non-adrenergic vasopressors found confidence intervals compatible with both no effect and clinically important benefit, justifying further RCTs. (PMID:39736782)
- Vasoplegic syndrome during heart transplantation lacks a formal definition, and studies of refractory cases are needed. (PMID:38428755)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Mortality benefit of second-line vasopressors beyond catecholamine sparing.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Add vasopressin to norepinephrine in refractory septic shock.
- Dopamine's higher mortality in septic shock.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Post-cardiopulmonary-bypass vasoplegia unresponsive to norepinephrine.

**Reviewer Gap**: Anaphylactic shock management (intramuscular adrenaline) is not covered in the retrieved sources and must be added.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31797234**: *Canadian Critical Care Society clinical practice guideline: The use of vasopressin and vasopressin analogues in critically ill adults with distributive shock* (2020) [guideline]
- **PMID 37548686**: *THE EFFICACY AND SAFETY OF VASOPRESSORS FOR SEPTIC SHOCK PATIENTS: A SYSTEMIC REVIEW AND NETWORK META-ANALYSIS* (2023) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 38428755**: *Vasoplegic syndrome during heart transplantation: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 38888542**: *ANGIOTENSIN II IN THE TREATMENT OF DISTRIBUTIVE SHOCK: A SYSTEMATIC-REVIEW AND META-ANALYSIS* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 39152516**: *The use of angiotensin II for the management of distributive shock: expert consensus statements* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 39736782**: *Non-adrenergic vasopressors for vasodilatory shock or perioperative vasoplegia: a meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials* (2024) [meta-analysis]

## [0561] Diuretic resistance in volume-overloaded ICU patients

**Cluster**: diuretic_resistance | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Diuretic resistance is poorly defined; appropriately high intravenous loop diuretic doses usually overcome it, and the goal is decongestion rather than protecting a creatinine value. Evidence comes largely from acute heart failure rather than ICU populations.

### Core Teaching Points
- Diuretic resistance is not accurately defined in acute heart failure and is uncommon with appropriately high IV loop diuretic doses; the aim should be decongestion rather than over-emphasising mild kidney biomarker changes. (PMID:40435978)
- Metolazone was as effective as chlorothiazide as an add-on to loop diuretics in acute decompensated heart failure, with comparable adverse events. (PMID:32639593)
- Combination diuretic strategies improved early decongestion without affecting prognosis, and SGLT2 inhibitor use during admission was associated with lower all-cause mortality and heart failure events. (PMID:41367306)
- Urinary sodium-guided diuretic therapy increased natriuresis and diuresis without more adverse events but had no proven effect on mortality, rehospitalisation or renal outcomes. (PMID:41571219)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal add-on agent; applicability of AHF data to ICU patients.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Add a thiazide-type diuretic to a loop diuretic for resistance.
- Using spot urinary sodium to judge diuretic response.

### Case Generation Hooks
- ICU patient on high-dose furosemide with persistent oliguria and fluid overload.

**Reviewer Gap**: ICU-specific evidence (renal replacement, ultrafiltration, albumin) is not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32639593**: *A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Metolazone Compared to Chlorothiazide for Treatment of Acute Decompensated Heart Failure* (2020) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40435978**: *Systematic Review on the Management of Diuretic Resistance in Acute Heart Failure across the Spectrum of Kidney Disease* (2025) [systematic review]
- **PMID 41367306**: *Combination diuretic therapy in acute heart failure: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 41571219**: *Urinary sodium-guided diuretic therapy in acute decompensated heart failure: A grade assessed systematic review and meta-analysis with trial sequential analysis* (2026) [meta-analysis]

## [0562] Diverticular Disease

**Cluster**: diverticular | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Uncomplicated left-sided diverticulitis can often be managed without antibiotics in selected patients, while surgery decisions for recurrent or complicated disease are individualised.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ACP suggests initially managing select patients with acute uncomplicated left-sided diverticulitis without antibiotics (conditional recommendation, low-certainty evidence). (PMID:35038273)
- For persistent, frequently recurring or complicated diverticulitis, the decision for elective surgery should be personalised after discussing benefits, harms, costs and patient preferences. (PMID:35038270)
- The 2025 EAES rapid guideline and the 2026 Fiesole global consensus provide current recommendations on surgical management of complicated diverticulitis and on diagnosis, management and prevention across health systems. (PMID:39733172, PMID:41443984)
- German guidelines separately cover pathogenesis and classification (Part I) and conservative, interventional and surgical management (Part II). (PMID:36411504, PMID:36459576)
- The 2024 IDSA update on complicated intra-abdominal infection includes diagnostic and antimicrobial recommendations. (PMID:38959299)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term outcomes of selective antibiotic use and surgical strategies are named research priorities.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Antibiotics are not always required in uncomplicated diverticulitis.
- Elective colectomy decisions after recurrence are individualised.

### Case Generation Hooks
- 45-year-old with left iliac fossa pain, CT-confirmed uncomplicated diverticulitis, no systemic upset.

**Reviewer Gap**: Hinchey classification, perforation management (Hartmann versus primary anastomosis) and follow-up colonoscopy are not stated in the abstracts retrieved.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35038270**: *Colonoscopy for Diagnostic Evaluation and Interventions to Prevent Recurrence After Acute Left-Sided Colonic Diverticulitis: A Clinical Guideline From the American College of Physicians* (2022) [guideline]
- **PMID 35038273**: *Diagnosis and Management of Acute Left-Sided Colonic Diverticulitis: A Clinical Guideline From the American College of Physicians* (2022) [guideline]
- **PMID 36411504**: *German guideline diverticular disease/diverticulitis: Part I: Methods, pathogenesis, epidemiology, clinical characteristics (definitions), natural course, diagnosis and classification* (2022) [guideline]
- **PMID 36459576**: *German guideline diverticular disease/diverticulitis: Part II: Conservative, interventional and surgical management* (2022) [guideline]
- **PMID 38959299**: *2024 Clinical Practice Guideline Update by the Infectious Diseases Society of America on Complicated Intra-abdominal Infections: Diagnostic Imaging of Suspected Acute Diverticulitis in Adults and Pregnant People* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 39733172**: *EAES rapid guideline: surgical management of complicated diverticulitis - with ESCP participation* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 41443984**: *Global guidelines on diverticular disease of the colon: the Fiesole Consensus report* (2026) [guideline]

## [0565] DNACPR, treatment escalation planning and advance care planning

**Cluster**: dnacpr | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
DNACPR and treatment escalation decisions belong inside advance care planning: patient-centred, balancing beneficence and autonomy, and carried through the perioperative period. Retrieved evidence is general rather than UK-statute specific.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ERC 2025 ethics guidelines emphasise a patient-centred approach and the balance between beneficence and autonomy in resuscitation decisions. (PMID:41117566)
- AAGBI/AoA guidance states perioperative implementation of advance care plans, including CPR plans, applies to all procedures however minor and regardless of anaesthetic technique. (PMID:35165886)
- A meta-analysis of RCTs in advanced cancer evaluated advance care planning; effects on dignity and care concordance need validation and optimal timing is unclear. (PMID:40378812)
- Asking patients to talk to their proxy or family about quality versus quantity of life can help initiate advance care planning. (PMID:38262171)
- Dyadic (patient and caregiver) advance care planning has been assessed for effect on end-of-life communication and decision-making, with barriers still to be defined. (PMID:38272378)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Best timing and format of advance care planning.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- A DNACPR decision is not a decision to withhold other treatment.
- Perioperative handling of an existing CPR plan.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Frail 86-year-old admitted with pneumonia: discussion of escalation limits and CPR.

**Reviewer Gap**: UK-specific DNACPR law (capacity, consultation, ReSPECT form) and recommended communication approach are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35165886**: *Implementing advance care plans in the peri-operative period, including plans for cardiopulmonary resuscitation: Association of Anaesthetists clinical practice guideline* (2022) [guideline]
- **PMID 38262171**: *Stages of readiness for advance care planning: Systematic review and meta-analysis of prevalence rates and associated factors* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 38272378**: *Effectiveness of Dyadic Advance Care Planning: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40378812**: *Effects of advance care planning for patients with advanced cancer: A meta-analysis of randomized controlled studies* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 41117566**: *European Resuscitation Council Guidelines 2025 Ethics in Resuscitation* (2025) [guideline]

## [0566] DOAC Reversal Agents

**Cluster**: doacs | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Direct oral anticoagulants are used without routine monitoring; the decisions that matter are perioperative interruption without bridging, specific reversal in life-threatening bleeding, and safe testing of drug levels.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2025 DOAC guidance states DOACs can be interrupted and resumed for procedures without bridging with LMWH, and names specific reversal agents for factor Xa inhibitors and idarucizumab for dabigatran, with prothrombin complex concentrates if these are unavailable. (PMID:40448969)
- The ISTH guidance reviews specific and non-specific reversal agents and states when reversal is indicated. (PMID:39029742)
- A clinical guideline on DOAC reversal in life-threatening bleeding could make no recommendation for recombinant factor VIIa as a non-specific haemostatic agent because of scant data. (PMID:38567679)
- A DOAC dipstick algorithm allows rapid exclusion of clinically relevant drug levels and distinguishes factor Xa from thrombin inhibitors where quantitative assays are unavailable. (PMID:38316416)
- The ACCP perioperative guideline notes that uncertainty remains about best practice for most perioperative antithrombotic questions. (PMID:35964704)
- In cirrhosis, anticoagulation is recommended for symptomatic portal vein thrombosis and suggested for asymptomatic progressing thrombosis. (PMID:38823454)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Evidence for non-specific haemostatic agents is limited.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Reversal agent for dabigatran (idarucizumab) versus factor Xa inhibitors.
- Why bridging is not needed for DOAC interruption.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Patient on apixaban with intracranial haemorrhage: reversal options.

**Reviewer Gap**: Dosing, renal adjustment and drug-drug interactions for each DOAC are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35964704**: *Perioperative Management of Antithrombotic Therapy: An American College of Chest Physicians Clinical Practice Guideline* (2022) [guideline]
- **PMID 38316416**: *Algorithm for Rapid Exclusion of Clinically Relevant Plasma Levels of Direct Oral Anticoagulants in Patients Using the DOAC Dipstick: An Expert Consensus Paper* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 38567679**: *Clinical guideline on reversal of direct oral anticoagulants in patients with life threatening bleeding* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 38823454**: *Anticoagulation for stroke prevention in atrial fibrillation and treatment of venous thromboembolism and portal vein thrombosis in cirrhosis: guidance from the SSC of the ISTH* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 39029742**: *Reversal of direct oral anticoagulants: guidance from the SSC of the ISTH* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 40448969**: *2025 Guidelines for direct oral anticoagulants: a practical guidance on the prescription, laboratory testing, peri-operative and bleeding management* (2025) [guideline]

## [0567] Donor-Specific Antibodies and Sensitisation

**Cluster**: dsa | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Donor-specific antibodies drive antibody-mediated rejection and graft loss, and sensitisation is increased by transfusion; monitoring and virtual crossmatching are the practical tools.

### Core Teaching Points
- Complement-activating donor-specific anti-HLA antibodies have a significant deleterious and independent prognostic impact on allograft loss and rejection. (PMID:37849755)
- De novo anti-HLA DSA is strongly associated with pancreas graft failure and rejection (OR 3.35), supporting post-transplant surveillance. (PMID:34772490)
- Blood transfusion after kidney transplantation was associated with a higher risk of immunological sensitisation. (PMID:36841513)
- A consensus conference developed recommendations on subclinical DSA monitoring in stable renal transplant recipients. (PMID:37560072)
- French guidelines allow omission of the wet crossmatch in selected recipients when virtual crossmatch results and clinical-biological agreement are strong. (PMID:40162484)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Clinical benefit of treating subclinical DSA.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Why transfusion matters for transplant candidates.
- Complement-fixing DSA carries worse prognosis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Renal transplant recipient with rising creatinine and new anti-HLA antibodies.

**Reviewer Gap**: Laboratory methods (single antigen beads, MFI thresholds) and treatment of antibody-mediated rejection are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34772490**: *Impact of Anti-HLA De Novo Donor Specific Antibody on Graft Outcomes in Pancreas Transplantation: A Meta-Analysis* (2021) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 36841513**: *Effect of blood transfusion post kidney transplantation on de novo human leukocytes antigen donor-specific antibody development and clinical outcomes in kidney transplant recipients: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2023) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 37560072**: *The Clinical Utility of Post-Transplant Monitoring of Donor-Specific Antibodies in Stable Renal Transplant Recipients: A Consensus Report With Guideline Statements for Clinical Practice* (2023) [guideline]
- **PMID 37849755**: *Complement-activating donor-specific anti-HLA antibodies in solid organ transplantation: systematic review, meta-analysis, and critical appraisal* (2023) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 40162484**: *Guidelines From the French-Speaking Society of Histocompatibility and Immunogenetics for Virtual Crossmatching for Deceased Donor Kidney Transplantation and the Use of Wet Crossmatch in the Decision-Making Process* (2025) [guideline]

## [0568] DRESS syndrome

**Cluster**: scar_dress | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Severe cutaneous adverse reactions are medical emergencies. Retrieved evidence concerns SJS/TEN, where early diagnosis, drug withdrawal, supportive care and monitoring for infection are central; DRESS-specific evidence was not retrieved.

### Core Teaching Points
- The German S3 guideline on epidermal necrolysis covers diagnosis, initial management and immunomodulating systemic therapy in Part 1, and supportive care and outpatient follow-up in Part 2. (PMID:39314017, PMID:39417754)
- Non-SCORTEN predictors of mortality in adult SJS/TEN include pre-existing renal disease, renal and respiratory involvement, diabetes, sepsis, comorbidities and time to hospitalisation. (PMID:39445692)
- Infectious complications are frequent in adult SJS/TEN and clinicians should monitor for and promptly investigate them during hospitalisation. (PMID:39431637)
- Cotrimoxazole-induced SCARs are associated with multiple HLA alleles, including HLA-A*11:01, HLA-B*13:01, HLA-B*15:02, HLA-B*38:02 and HLA-C*08:01. (PMID:38568509)
- Paediatric SJS/TEN guidelines are heterogeneous: supportive care is emphasised but pharmacologic and long-term guidance is inconsistent. (PMID:41698348)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Evidence for any specific immunomodulatory drug in SJS/TEN is limited and guideline-dependent.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Stop the culprit drug immediately in SJS/TEN.
- HLA-B*15:02 and carbamazepine-type pharmacogenomic screening logic.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Two weeks after starting an antiepileptic, a patient develops fever, mucosal erosions and blistering skin.

**Reviewer Gap**: DRESS (RegiSCAR criteria, eosinophilia, steroid use, long latency) and benign drug eruptions are not covered by the retrieved abstracts; topics 568, 576 and 577 need a DRESS source before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38568509**: *Human Leukocyte Antigens and Sulfamethoxazole/Cotrimoxazole-Induced Severe Cutaneous Adverse Reactions: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 39314017**: *S3 guideline: Diagnosis and treatment of epidermal necrolysis (Stevens-Johnson syndrome and toxic epidermal necrolysis) - Part 1: Diagnosis, initial management, and immunomodulating systemic therapy* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 39417754**: *S3 guideline: Diagnosis and treatment of epidermal necrolysis (Stevens-Johnson syndrome and toxic epidermal necrolysis) - Part 2: Supportive therapy of EN in the acute and post-acute stages* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 39431637**: *Infectious complications of Stevens-Johnson syndrome and toxic epidermal necrolysis: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 39445692**: *Systematic review and meta-analysis of non-SCORTEN predictors of mortality in Stevens-Johnson syndrome and toxic epidermal necrolysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 41698348**: *Diagnosis and Management of Stevens-Johnson Syndrome and Toxic Epidermal Necrolysis in Pediatric Patients: A Systematic Review of Clinical Guidelines and Consensus Statements* (2026) [guideline]

## [0569] Drug allergy assessment and penicillin allergy de-labelling

**Cluster**: penicillin_allergy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Most patients labelled penicillin-allergic are not allergic. Delabelling by direct oral challenge or structured testing is safe, can be done by non-allergists, and improves antibiotic choice.

### Core Teaching Points
- Penicillin allergy delabelling by non-allergists is efficacious and safe, with reported harm about 0% (95% CI 0-1%); the proportion delabelled rises with the complexity of testing, but many can be delabelled without skin testing. (PMID:36450321)
- Reactions to direct penicillin challenges are infrequent and comparable to indirect challenges after allergy testing, so direct challenge is safe across age groups and settings. (PMID:39283610)
- BSACI guidance supports delabelling services run by non-allergists for low-risk patients with a framework for drug provocation testing, with separate adult and paediatric sections. (PMID:36128691)
- Inaccurate allergy labels should be removed after a negative drug provocation test with proper counselling. (PMID:36133403)
- Surgical patients with a penicillin allergy label can be assessed and tested by trained professionals meeting minimum safe-testing standards. (PMID:30916014)
- The reported global prevalence of penicillin allergy comes mainly from high-income countries and secondary care. (PMID:39874990)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Data from low-income settings.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Direct oral amoxicillin challenge for low-risk histories.
- Why delabelling improves antimicrobial stewardship.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Patient with childhood rash on amoxicillin needing perioperative cefazolin.

**Reviewer Gap**: Risk-stratification tools (PEN-FAST), management of severe delayed reactions and cross-reactivity with cephalosporins are not stated in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30916014**: *Management of a surgical patient with a label of penicillin allergy: narrative review and consensus recommendations* (2019) [guideline]
- **PMID 36128691**: *BSACI guideline for the set-up of penicillin allergy de-labelling services by non-allergists working in a hospital setting* (2022) [guideline]
- **PMID 36133403**: *Hong Kong Drug Allergy Delabelling Initiative (HK-DADI) consensus statements for penicillin allergy testing by nonallergists* (2022) [guideline]
- **PMID 36450321**: *The effectiveness of interventions that support penicillin allergy assessment and delabeling of adult and pediatric patients by nonallergy specialists: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2023) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 39283610**: *Reaction Risk to Direct Penicillin Challenges: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis* (2024) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 39874990**: *The global prevalence of reported penicillin allergy: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]

## [0570] Drug Dosing in Renal Impairment

**Cluster**: renal_dosing | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Drug dosing in renal impairment follows kidney function, drug properties and monitoring; non-adherence to renal dosing recommendations causes harm and extra health-service use.

### Core Teaching Points
- Non-adherence to renal dosing recommendations in CKD was associated with adverse clinical consequences and increased healthcare utilisation. (PMID:42590882)
- Renal impairment is the most significant risk factor for cefepime-induced neurotoxicity; continuous infusion, dose adjustment and therapeutic drug monitoring are mitigation strategies. (PMID:40523836)
- The KDIGO 2024 CKD guideline gives pharmacists a role in medication management, nephrotoxin stewardship and dose adjustment. (PMID:40197825)
- An ADQI consensus addresses preventing anti-cancer drug nephrotoxicity and enabling optimal drug dosing in kidney disease. (PMID:41361704)
- A Chinese consensus advises on selection and dose adjustment of chemotherapy, small molecules and antibodies in patients with cancer and CKD. (PMID:38679791)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Which estimate of kidney function (eGFR or Cockcroft-Gault) should drive dosing.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Neurotoxicity of beta-lactams (cefepime) in renal impairment.
- Dose adjustment is a safety, not optional, step.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Elderly patient with eGFR 20 on cefepime who becomes encephalopathic.

**Reviewer Gap**: Drug-by-drug dose tables and the eGFR versus creatinine-clearance debate are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38679791**: *[Chinese expert consensus on the guidance of anti-tumor drugs for patients with tumor combined chronic kidney disease (2024 edition)]* (2024) [guideline]
- **PMID 40197825**: *KDIGO 2024 clinical practice guideline on evaluation and management of chronic kidney disease: A primer on what pharmacists need to know* (2025) [guideline]
- **PMID 40523836**: *Unveiling predisposing factors for cefepime-induced neurotoxicity: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 41361704**: *The nephrotoxic effects of anti-cancer therapies: consensus report of the 34th Acute Disease Quality Initiative workgroup* (2026) [guideline]
- **PMID 42590882**: *Current practice and guideline adherence in renal dose adjustment for patients with chronic kidney disease: A systematic review and meta-analysis* (2026) [guideline]

## [0571] Drug eruptions and severe cutaneous adverse reactions (SCAR)

**Cluster**: scar_dress | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Severe cutaneous adverse reactions are medical emergencies. Retrieved evidence concerns SJS/TEN, where early diagnosis, drug withdrawal, supportive care and monitoring for infection are central; DRESS-specific evidence was not retrieved.

### Core Teaching Points
- The German S3 guideline on epidermal necrolysis covers diagnosis, initial management and immunomodulating systemic therapy in Part 1, and supportive care and outpatient follow-up in Part 2. (PMID:39314017, PMID:39417754)
- Non-SCORTEN predictors of mortality in adult SJS/TEN include pre-existing renal disease, renal and respiratory involvement, diabetes, sepsis, comorbidities and time to hospitalisation. (PMID:39445692)
- Infectious complications are frequent in adult SJS/TEN and clinicians should monitor for and promptly investigate them during hospitalisation. (PMID:39431637)
- Cotrimoxazole-induced SCARs are associated with multiple HLA alleles, including HLA-A*11:01, HLA-B*13:01, HLA-B*15:02, HLA-B*38:02 and HLA-C*08:01. (PMID:38568509)
- Paediatric SJS/TEN guidelines are heterogeneous: supportive care is emphasised but pharmacologic and long-term guidance is inconsistent. (PMID:41698348)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Evidence for any specific immunomodulatory drug in SJS/TEN is limited and guideline-dependent.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Stop the culprit drug immediately in SJS/TEN.
- HLA-B*15:02 and carbamazepine-type pharmacogenomic screening logic.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Two weeks after starting an antiepileptic, a patient develops fever, mucosal erosions and blistering skin.

**Reviewer Gap**: DRESS (RegiSCAR criteria, eosinophilia, steroid use, long latency) and benign drug eruptions are not covered by the retrieved abstracts; topics 568, 576 and 577 need a DRESS source before approval.

## [0572] Drug-induced acute interstitial nephritis

**Cluster**: drug_ain | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Drug-induced acute interstitial nephritis is an under-recognised cause of acute kidney injury with several common culprits (PPIs, beta-lactams, 5-ASA, immune checkpoint inhibitors). Stopping the drug and, in selected cases, giving steroids is the mainstay.

### Core Teaching Points
- Proton pump inhibitor use was associated with adverse kidney outcomes including AIN (RR 3.61), though from low-quality observational data. (PMID:28339835)
- AIN is the most common cause of immune checkpoint inhibitor-related kidney injury and is often treated with steroids; the need for biopsy is controversial. (PMID:37453935)
- French shared guidelines for ICI-associated AKI vary steroid treatment with severity and renal response, note a lack of strong data for steroid-resistant cases, and discuss rechallenge, which appears to carry a good renal prognosis. (PMID:39643454)
- In 5-ASA-induced interstitial nephritis in IBD, at least six-monthly monitoring of renal function is suggested because early steroid treatment may be beneficial. (PMID:35488310)
- Vedolizumab can rarely cause AIN in IBD, so symptoms and laboratory findings consistent with AIN warrant monitoring of kidney function. (PMID:38682791)
- Cefazolin is a first-line option for MSSA infections, with fewer discontinuations for side effects than antistaphylococcal penicillins. (PMID:29437617)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Role of biopsy and steroid duration in AIN.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Common drug causes of AIN: PPI, beta-lactams, NSAIDs, 5-ASA, ICIs.
- Management: withdraw drug; steroids in selected cases.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Patient on a checkpoint inhibitor and omeprazole with rising creatinine and sterile pyuria.

**Reviewer Gap**: Classic triad, urinary eosinophils and biopsy findings are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28339835**: *The association between proton pump inhibitor use and the risk of adverse kidney outcomes: a systematic review and meta-analysis* (2018) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 29437617**: *Systematic Review and Meta-analysis of the Safety of Antistaphylococcal Penicillins Compared to Cefazolin* (2018) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 35488310**: *5-ASA induced interstitial nephritis in patients with inflammatory bowel disease: a systematic review* (2022) [systematic review]
- **PMID 37453935**: *Chapter 3: Management of kidney injury caused by cancer drug therapy, from clinical practice guidelines for the management of kidney injury during anticancer drug therapy 2022* (2023) [guideline]
- **PMID 38682791**: *Acute interstitial nephritis in patients with inflammatory bowel disease treated with vedolizumab: a systematic review* (2024) [systematic review]
- **PMID 39643454**: *[Acute kidney injury in cancer patients receiving immune checkpoint inhibitor therapy-shared guidelines of FITC/SFNDT]* (2025) [guideline]

## [0573] Drug-Induced ILD

**Cluster**: drug_ild | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Drug-induced interstitial lung disease, especially from anticancer drugs, is diagnosed by exclusion with multidisciplinary input. High-quality evidence is lacking and steroid regimens differ between consensus documents.

### Core Teaching Points
- A systematic review found glucocorticoids commonly used for drug-induced ILD but no prospective studies of their effect on outcome; overall high-quality evidence is lacking. (PMID:30326612)
- Expert consensus on anticancer drug-induced ILD gives recommendations for early screening, diagnosis and treatment with an algorithm, and stresses multidisciplinary collaboration. (PMID:36867358, PMID:35880334)
- A critical appraisal of guidelines found inconsistencies between documents, notably in steroid regimens, and called for biomarker research. (PMID:38323333)
- Immune checkpoint inhibitor pneumonitis needs enhanced recognition and timely individualised management. (PMID:40768795)
- JAK inhibitors increased non-opportunistic respiratory infections versus placebo, but the risk of serious pulmonary adverse events was low. (PMID:32406509)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal steroid dose and duration; ILD risk from ICIs lacks randomised data.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Stop the offending drug first; steroids for symptomatic or severe cases.
- Anticancer drugs most associated with ILD.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Patient on an EGFR tyrosine kinase inhibitor with new dry cough and ground-glass opacities.

**Reviewer Gap**: Typical culprit drugs beyond cancer therapy (amiodarone, nitrofurantoin, methotrexate), imaging patterns and grading are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30326612**: *Drug-Induced Interstitial Lung Disease: A Systematic Review* (2018) [systematic review]
- **PMID 32406509**: *Pulmonary adverse events of small molecule JAK inhibitors in autoimmune disease: systematic review and meta-analysis* (2020) [meta-analysis]
- **PMID 35880334**: *[Expert consensus on the diagnosis and treatment of anticancer drug-induced interstitial lung disease]* (2022) [guideline]
- **PMID 36867358**: *Expert Consensus on the Diagnosis and Treatment of Anticancer Drug-Induced Interstitial Lung Disease* (2023) [guideline]
- **PMID 38323333**: *Anticancer drug-induced interstitial lung disease: a critical appraisal of clinical practice guidelines and consensus statements* (2026) [guideline]
- **PMID 40768795**: *Immune checkpoint inhibitor-related pneumonitis: From guidelines to the front lines* (2025) [guideline]

## [0574] Drug-Induced Lupus Erythematosus

**Cluster**: drug_lupus | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Drug-induced lupus usually resolves after the culprit drug is withdrawn, and a careful drug history with temporal association is the key diagnostic step. Retrieved evidence is limited.

### Core Teaching Points
- It is important to recognise culprit drugs because discontinuation usually improves drug-induced lupus manifestations. (PMID:29870500)
- In children with SLE-like features, a proper drug history is crucial; association with a drug and resolution of symptoms on withdrawal supports the diagnosis. (PMID:38580326)
- In anti-TNF-treated patients with IBD and joint symptoms, a drug-induced lupus-like syndrome should be considered. (PMID:31650573)
- Classic drug-induced lupus agents can enhance innate immune responses, including neutrophil extracellular trap formation and exposure of autoantigens. (PMID:30005854)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Mechanisms are incompletely understood.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Resolution on drug withdrawal as the diagnostic clue.
- Anti-TNF agents as a cause of lupus-like syndrome.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Patient on long-term hydralazine with arthralgia, fever and pleuritic pain.

**Reviewer Gap**: Culprit drug list, typical antihistone antibody pattern, and the contrast with SLE (renal and CNS sparing) are not in the retrieved abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29870500**: *Drug-induced lupus erythematosus: an update on drugs and mechanisms* (2018) [review]
- **PMID 30005854**: *Drug-induced lupus: Traditional and new concepts* (2018) [review]
- **PMID 31650573**: *Expert consensus: practical algorithms for management of inflammatory bowel disease patients presenting with back pain or peripheral arthropathies* (2019) [guideline]
- **PMID 38580326**: *Drug-induced lupus erythematosus in childhood: Case-based review* (2024) [systematic review]
